2 The Foundation Model

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why does a capable model still need a precise contract around every call?

A model call can return a response that parses cleanly yet violates the tool schema, arrives past its deadline, or claims a file edit succeeded before any tool has run. Because every capability an agent adds, from tool dispatch and memory retrieval to sandboxed execution and recovery, is built from individual model calls, any ambiguity at the call boundary spreads into every subsystem downstream. A foundation model is an ungrounded token predictor that emits candidate proposals across an untrusted boundary, not a verified state transition. The surrounding runtime must therefore enforce reliability at each call through token budgets, schema checks, and independent verification. Within the H·S·A exposure space, the single call sits at the origin: one bounded turn, zero carried state, and zero ambient authority.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain the separate responsibilities of the foundation model, inference service, and agent runtime
  • Construct a token-bounded request containing task messages, advertised tools, and generation limits
  • Calculate how temperature and nucleus truncation reshape candidates without establishing correctness, and explain why only a recorded call replays exactly
  • Diagnose response outcomes using completion status, structural validity, permission checks, and external evidence that self-review cannot supply
  • Distinguish the guarantees of grammar-constrained decoding from learned formatting and correct task values
  • Estimate call latency and repeated-input work under explicit serving and prefix-reuse assumptions
  • Evaluate interface representations using accepted outcomes and resource use under matched task budgets
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

2.1 One Turn of the Repair Loop

A single call runs one turn, carries no state, and holds zero ambient authority.

Yao, Shunyu, Jeffrey Zhao, Dian Yu, Nan Du, Izhak Shafran, Karthik Narasimhan, and Yuan Cao. 2023. “ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

The repair agent (Four lighthouse workloads) needs to inspect the connection-pool implementation before proposing a change. A model call that emits read_file("src/pool.py") produces only an unexecuted text string. Of the six phases of a turn (The six phases of a turn), only the call itself belongs to the model; checking the path, authorizing the read, running it, and returning the observation all belong to the runtime. This loop of model-generated proposals and runtime observations is the interaction pattern formalized by ReAct (Yao et al. 2023), and every runtime phase depends on knowing what the single model phase delivers.

In a chat window, a human reader spots a response cut off mid-sentence, judges whether a shell command is safe to run, and waits out variable generation delays. An autonomous agent removes that human reader. The runtime receives the response as data and must check in code what a person would judge by eye: whether the prompt fit inside the context window, whether generation finished normally or hit the output token limit, whether a well-formed tool request targets a path inside the allowed workspace, and how much time and budget the call spent. Raw reply text cannot answer those questions; a runtime that guesses call state by scraping output strings will eventually apply a truncated patch or run a command outside its workspace.

Preventing those failures requires an explicit call contract between the agent runtime and the inference service. That contract covers both directions of the call: the outbound request (message history, advertised tool definitions, and token and time limits) and the inbound response record (payload kind, stop reason, normalized status, and token usage). It also fixes the checks the runtime runs before dispatching any proposed action, along with the latency and input-recomputation costs of each call. Rather than assuming the model is reliable, the contract treats the inference service as an untrusted, probabilistic generator and keeps every control-flow decision in the runtime.

The chapter follows the repair agent’s single inspection call from request to bill, adding one runtime obligation at each step. Before the call is sent, the runtime must know who owns which decision (section 2.2) and whether the serialized request fits with room left for an answer (section 2.3). How the service then produces a candidate (section 2.4) explains why repeated calls differ and why generation can stop before the candidate is whole. That possibility fixes the order of the checks on the way back. The runtime first reads the call’s normalized status to learn whether a complete candidate arrived at all (section 2.5), then asks which property each check on a completed candidate actually establishes (section 2.6), and finally whether syntax errors can be prevented during generation instead of rejected after it (section 2.7). The last two sections price the call. Output length and re-sent history set its latency and work (section 2.8), and those costs decide how competing output representations should be compared, on accepted repairs under a matched budget (section 2.9).

2.2 The Model Call Boundary

If a runtime treats a model’s output string read_file("src/pool.py") as an already-authorized file read, a single hallucinated path can read outside the workspace before any check runs. Keeping a proposed action separate from an executed one requires splitting each call across three components: the foundation model, the inference service, and the agent runtime. The foundation model1 computes conditional probability distributions over token sequences given a prompt context. The inference service hosts the model weights, runs forward passes on accelerators, applies sampling parameters, and packages the output tokens into a response record. The agent runtime drives the task: it builds the prompt, tracks budgets, checks candidate outputs, and decides whether an action is allowed to run in the host environment. Even when all three run in the same process on one machine, their interfaces keep generated text separate from the decision to act on it.

1 Homogenization: The same report that named foundation models warned that, because many applications adapt one shared base model, a defect in that model passes into every system built on it (Bommasani et al. 2021). For autonomous agents the warning is concrete. Every runtime that invokes the same model and serving path inherits the same failure modes at once, so the runtime, rather than the model, must hold the operational checks.

Bommasani, Rishi, Drew A. Hudson, Ehsan Adeli, Russ Altman, Simran Arora, Sydney von Arx, Michael S. Bernstein, et al. 2021. “On the Opportunities and Risks of Foundation Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2108.07258.
Definition 2.1: Proposal

A proposal is model output that specifies a candidate action or answer for the runtime to evaluate, carrying zero ambient authority and producing no external side effects until the runtime explicitly validates, authorizes, and executes it.

  1. Significance: Every external effect of an agent depends on an explicit runtime decision. Because the model call possesses zero ambient authority, a rejected proposal costs one inference invocation rather than an unintended modification to the environment. That invocation still consumes resources and counts toward \(\AttemptCost\) in the cost per accepted task (equation 1.2).
  2. Distinction: A proposal must not be confused with an effect (a state change executed by the runtime after authorization) or an observation (the runtime’s factual record of what an operation returned). Emitting read_file("src/pool.py") is merely a proposal; the file contents subsequently retrieved from disk and supplied to the next call constitute an observation.
  3. Common pitfall: Logging a tool request as an executed action, or accepting a model’s textual claim that “the file has been read” as an empirical observation, allows fluent prose to stand in for operational evidence.

2.2.1 Messages and tool definitions

A model cannot tell an operator’s instruction, a user’s prompt, and a file excerpt returned by a tool apart unless the runtime labels each piece of the request, yet those labels alone do not make the content trustworthy. The runtime organizes the prompt as an ordered sequence of role-tagged messages: a system message sets task instructions and constraints, a user message supplies the task prompt, an assistant message records earlier model outputs, and a tool message carries the raw observation returned when an action runs. Role tags organize the token stream for the model’s attention mechanism, while the runtime remains responsible for checking whether the content is authentic and follows the task contract.

↩︎ The task contract, which fixes the goal, environment, permitted actions, available observations, and completion criteria, is established in The five-part task contract.

Alongside the message history, the request advertises the tools available to the model and gives their argument schemas. In listing 1, the runtime declares a file-reading tool with one required string parameter. The JSON-schema definition tells the model what syntax a valid proposal needs, while the runtime enforces workspace sandboxing and filesystem permissions independently.

The model_config field binds the request to a specific model version, weights checkpoint, and serving profile, while sampling controls how the service draws output tokens from the model’s predicted distribution. Two resource bounds protect the call from running away: max_output_tokens (\(\OutputLimit\)) caps how many tokens the call can generate, and deadline (\(\CompletionDeadline\)) sets a timeout against queue delays and serving stalls. Writing those bounds into the request works only when the inference service or client runtime enforces them during generation.

When generation finishes, the inference service returns free-form text, a tool proposal, or a service error, and a runtime adapter normalizes that provider-specific reply into a single envelope (listing 2). Here, the service reports a completed generation pass that produced a tool request to read src/pool.py, one diagnostic step before any repair is checked.

Here, kind tells the runtime parser how to read the payload. The normalized status confirms that transport and generation finished under the adapter’s contract, while stop_reason records the stop reason reported by the serving engine: whether the model emitted a tool-call marker, hit an end-of-sequence token, or stopped at the token limit. The usage block counts \(\SequenceLength\) input tokens read and \(\OutputLength\) output tokens generated. Bare \(\SequenceLength\) is sequence length throughout this book, here the prompt a call reads; the State levels \(\Glyph{S}_0\) to \(\Glyph{S}_3\) always carry their subscript. Tracking usage across calls prevents context-window overflow and feeds cost accounting; if a provider omits token counts, the runtime records usage as missing rather than zero.

Every path out of the response ends in the runtime, and the stop reason chooses the path (figure 1). A tool call goes to validation and authorization before anything runs, a finished answer goes to verification, and output cut off at its limit goes to quarantine, where it is kept for diagnosis and never executed (section 2.5.2 states the rule).

2.2.2 Proposals and observations

Following the tool-call branch of figure 1 turns that returned read_file proposal into an observation without trusting its text. Because the payload could still carry a traversal string like ../../etc/passwd or a malformed argument, the runtime first checks structural conformance, confirming that the proposed arguments match the declared schema (for example, that path is a string rather than a shell command). Next, it checks the arguments against the task’s permission grant: advertising read_file lets the model propose file reads, but grants no right to read arbitrary paths. The runtime verifies that src/pool.py resolves inside the allowed workspace rather than escaping to host files through directory traversal. Once authorized, the tool runs outside the model call, inside an isolated sandbox or container with its own timeout.

After the tool finishes, the runtime records what happened and builds the next prompt. It appends the model’s assistant proposal paired with a tool message carrying the observation (here, the bounded file excerpt). That observation reports the actual outcome, including a missing file or a permission denial, and notes when the output was truncated; One mediated round trip develops how the runtime matches each result to the call it answers, records errors, and marks truncation.

The model on the next turn sees the tool output only because the runtime rebuilds and sends the updated context window. Foundation models are stateless token predictors with no internal memory of earlier requests or tool runs. Session identifiers or server-side prefix caching (reusing attention state the service already computed for a prompt prefix that repeats across turns, priced in section 2.8.3) can cut latency, but the runtime alone decides which messages enter the prompt. That separation also means an observation can go stale: a file excerpt in the prompt shows the file only as it stood when read, so if a later command edits the file on disk, the excerpt in context is out of date.

The gap between a proposal and an observation is where the fail-plausible fault enters. A model can emit a well-formed proposal targeting the wrong source module, or a convincing analysis grounded in an obsolete observation, and the tokens look the same either way. Agent engineering therefore requires treating status, schema structure, security authorization, and empirical evidence as distinct, non-fungible properties of the interaction. Table 1 summarizes what the runtime owns around every call, whether the model runs in the local process or behind a remote API.

↩︎ The fail-plausible fault, output that looks well formed and credible while the intended property fails, is established in The fail-plausible fault.

The request and response records fix the boundary around a model call: messages supply context, tool definitions specify what the model may propose, and the runtime mediates every effect on external state. Before the service can run the call, however, the whole request must fit inside the model’s context window with room left for the response, and checking that fit requires counting tokens rather than characters, messages, or files.

2.3 Tokenizing the Request

Once the repair agent appends the src/pool.py excerpt and a compiler diagnostic to its message history, every piece of the request (role delimiters, tool schemas, system instructions, file paths, and source lines) serializes into a single sequence of discrete tokens before entering the model. Whether the next call fits the context window depends on the token count of that fully assembled sequence, plus the output room reserved for the reply, measured strictly against the target model’s tokenizer and wire format.

2.3.1 Tokens in the serialized request

Leading indentation or an unusual identifier in src/pool.py can change the prompt’s token count even when a character counter or source-code lexer sees the same program structure, because a model’s tokenizer slices text into vocabulary identifiers whose boundaries rarely match words, identifiers, or JSON fields. Subword methods represent common fragments compactly while splitting unfamiliar words and identifiers into smaller pieces (Sennrich et al. 2016). Byte-pair encoding (BPE)2 learns repeated combinations of smaller units; other tokenizers use different segmentation rules, and some learn them directly from raw text without assuming word boundaries (Kudo and Richardson 2018). Because a single token may represent a word, part of a word, whitespace, punctuation, or a combination of these, small formatting changes alter the token sequence.

2 Byte-Pair Encoding (BPE): The algorithm began as a data-compression scheme that repeatedly replaces the most frequent pair of adjacent bytes with an unused symbol (Gage 1994); Sennrich et al. (2016) repurposed the same merge rule to build open vocabularies for neural translation. Because the merges are learned from the training corpus’s frequencies, the token cost of a string reflects how common it was in that corpus, so an unusual identifier or path can split into more tokens than a common word of the same length, and the runtime must count rather than estimate.

Gage, Philip. 1994. “A New Algorithm for Data Compression.” C Users Journal 12 (2): 23–38.
Sennrich, Rico, Barry Haddow, and Alexandra Birch. 2016. “Neural Machine Translation of Rare Words with Subword Units.” Proceedings of the 54th Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics (Volume 1: Long Papers), 1715–25. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/P16-1162.
Kudo, Taku, and John Richardson. 2018. “SentencePiece: A Simple and Language Independent Subword Tokenizer and Detokenizer for Neural Text Processing.” Proceedings of the 2018 Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing: System Demonstrations, 66–71. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/D18-2012.

Indentation alone alters the resulting sequence. Under one widely used byte-pair vocabulary, cl100k_base, for example, the unindented line def get_user_id(): encodes as five tokens beginning with def (identifier 755). Indenting that exact line by four spaces yields six tokens: three leading spaces (262), followed by def with the fourth space fused to the keyword (711), and finally the remaining four tokens. A compiler lexer records indentation as block structure and emits the same keyword token at both levels; the model instead receives a different opening sequence of integer identifiers, with only the four tokens after the keyword shared (figure 2).

In an inspection request, the string src/pool.py is a single path argument at the tool interface, yet it splits into several subword tokens at the model interface. Changing a file path, adding indentation, or escaping source code inside JSON shifts token boundaries in ways a fixed character-to-token ratio cannot predict. Neither JSON framing nor punctuation adds a constant fraction of tokens over plain prose, so the runtime must tokenize the exact byte sequence it plans to send.

Wire framing adds further tokens beyond the visible text. Model APIs wrap messages in control tokens that mark roles, turn boundaries, and tool schemas. Counting only the user prompt or tool output misses that wrapper. When the runtime formats the wire payload locally, it must run the model’s own tokenizer over the complete formatted sequence; when a remote service applies its own chat template or hidden system wrapper, the runtime must use the provider’s token-counting endpoint and admission rules. Because vocabularies and merge tables differ across model families, a token count from one model does not carry over to another.

The same count applies to generated output. A compact tool call uses fewer output tokens than a long prose explanation wrapping the same call, though the exact count depends on how the call is serialized. Shorter schemas cut latency and token spend only when they keep the fields the runtime needs; dropping required arguments, file paths, or error variants to save a few tokens makes downstream execution brittle.

2.3.2 Output headroom

A prompt that fits inside the context window can still leave too little room for the model to finish its reply, cutting off a tool call mid-argument. To prevent a long prompt from starving the response, the admission check must reserve output headroom before sending the call. Let \(\SequenceLength\) denote the length of the fully serialized input prompt, \(\ContextWindow\) the total context window (the maximum sequence capacity spanning input and output tokens within a single invocation), and \(\OutputLimit\) the reserved generation allowance. Where input tokens and generated tokens share a unified context budget, an admission check must enforce:

\[\SequenceLength \le \ContextWindow - \OutputLimit \tag{1}\]

This inequality guarantees that an incoming prompt cannot fill the context buffer and leave the model no room to answer. Many services also cap input and output lengths separately alongside the shared window, so the runtime must satisfy both the individual caps and the combined bound. Reasoning models add another wrinkle: they emit intermediate reasoning tokens before the visible answer, and those hidden tokens count against \(\OutputLimit\) even when the caller never sees them. A three-line visible reply can exhaust a generous output allowance if the model spent hundreds of hidden reasoning tokens first.

↪ How reasoning models spend generated tokens on intermediate work, and how the runtime budgets them, is developed in How reasoning models spend output allowances.

Suppose the repair interface has a combined window of 8,192 tokens, and the runtime reserves 1,024 tokens for generation. Its input ceiling is \(\ContextWindow - \OutputLimit = 8,192 - 1,024 = 7,168\) tokens. If the fully serialized request occupies 6,000 tokens, \(7,168 - 6,000 = 1,168\) tokens of room remain for additional input while preserving the response allowance. The limits are scenario values, not a named model’s specification.

If an incoming tool observation exceeds this remaining headroom, the admission check fails. The runtime can then compress prompt history, extract a narrower file slice, or route the call to an execution profile with a larger context window. Shrinking the output allowance \(\OutputLimit\) to force admission is risky, as it leaves the model insufficient room to complete complex tool invocations or structured plans. Similarly, silently truncating tool outputs to squeeze them into the remaining margin can strip away the critical stack trace or diagnostic error that the agent needs to resolve the bug.

Even when a request satisfies admission checks, generation can still hit the output token ceiling mid-stream. The runtime must never confuse a syntactically well-formed prefix with a successfully terminated generation. A truncated JSON object may happen to pass a lenient parser if closed artificially, yet it remains an incomplete execution plan. While a partial payload provides valuable diagnostic telemetry, executing it as an intact tool call can cause downstream corruption. The invocation contract in section 2.5 turns this boundary into an explicit rule that reads the call’s status before its payload.

2.3.3 Counting multimodal inputs

Attaching a screenshot of a failing dashboard or user-interface (UI) render to a repair request can push a prompt that fit in text well past the context limit. A vision front-end maps each image into a sequence of context positions, and although vision transformers slice images into uniform patches (Dosovitskiy et al. 2021), preprocessing steps such as dynamic tiling, resolution scaling, and spatial pooling mean the billed token count rarely equals a simple patch count.

Dosovitskiy, Alexey, Lucas Beyer, Alexander Kolesnikov, Dirk Weissenborn, Xiaohua Zhai, Thomas Unterthiner, Mostafa Dehghani, et al. 2021. “An Image Is Worth 16x16 Words: Transformers for Image Recognition at Scale.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

Consider the earlier budget when adding this visual artifact. If the service reports that the image and its framing tokens require an additional 2,048 context positions, the total request demand expands to \(6,000 + 2,048 + 1,024 = 9,072\) tokens. This total exceeds the 8,192-token context window by 880 tokens, triggering an immediate admission failure before dispatch. The runtime must step in before calling the model. Downscaling the image cuts its token count under some vision backends, but heavy compression can blur single-character error codes or fine trace lines. Budgeting multimodal inputs therefore trades visual detail against the context ceiling.

Checkpoint 2.1: Admitting the next repair request

The repair agent is about to add a source excerpt and a diagnostic image to its next call. Its output allowance must remain available for a complete proposal.

Token accounting checks upfront that the full request fits with room reserved for the reply. Admission, however, covers only starting capacity. Once decoding begins, token-by-token autoregressive generation determines how that reserved headroom is spent and whether the call finishes cleanly or stops mid-stream.

2.4 Autoregressive Generation

Even when a request fixes the conversation history and the list of available tool schemas, the model’s reply remains open-ended and stochastic. On the same repair prompt, one call might propose read_file on a configuration path, a second might inspect a different directory, and a third might return prose instead of a tool call. Understanding how the serving engine generates tokens one step at a time explains how to set token budgets and sampling parameters, why repeating a live API call can produce a different trajectory, and why only replaying a recorded response reproduces downstream execution exactly.

2.4.1 Token-by-token generation

When a model emits a file path inside a read_file argument, it chooses each token from learned statistical patterns without checking the filesystem, and that early choice conditions every closing delimiter and reasoning token that follows. Autoregressive language models generate text by factoring the joint probability of a sequence into a product of conditional next-token distributions (Bengio et al. 2003). Let \(\InputTokenSequence\) denote the input tokens and \(\OutputTokenSequence\) an output sequence. Its conditional probability factors as

\[\Pr(\OutputTokenSequence \mid \InputTokenSequence) = \prod_{t=1}^{\OutputLength} \Pr(\OutputToken_t \mid \InputTokenSequence, \OutputTokenPrefix) \tag{2}\]

At output position \(\TokenIndex\), the model with parameters \(\ModelParameters\) produces one score, or logit, for each token in vocabulary \(\TokenVocabulary\):

\[\LogitVector_t = \NextTokenLogitFunction(\InputTokenSequence, \OutputTokenPrefix) \in \mathbb{R}^{|\TokenVocabulary|}\]

A decoding rule selects a token from these scores and appends it to the prefix for position \(\TokenIndex+1\), so early token choices cascade through the rest of the sequence. In an inference serving engine, the decode loop reads the prompt, computes next-token logits, picks an output token, appends it to the prefix, and repeats until a stop condition fires. Generation ends normally when the model emits an end-of-sequence token or matches a caller-supplied stop sequence. It can also stop early at the output token ceiling, or abort on a timeout or network drop. Serving interfaces must report which stop condition fired: an incomplete JSON object cut off at the token limit needs different handling than a finished tool call, and even a clean stop sequence says only that generation ended, not that the task succeeded.

Production Transformer implementations (Vaswani et al. 2017) avoid recomputing earlier tokens at every decode step by keeping a key-value (KV) cache:3 memory buffers holding the attention keys and values already computed for earlier positions. For a given full-attention architecture and precision, keeping more tokens in flight takes more cache memory. That cache is transient serving state, not an audit log of which tools ran or what they changed. KV Cache Management covers cache layout and sizing; for a single call, the key point is that both prompt tokens and generated tokens occupy memory during the call.

3 Key-Value (KV) Cache: In causal attention, key and value vectors at a given position depend solely on that token and its predecessors, so once computed they remain static throughout subsequent sequence expansion. Retaining them in device memory turns each new decode step from full-sequence recomputation into a single new row projection per layer alongside a read of stored rows. Consequently, cache capacity and memory bandwidth, rather than floating-point arithmetic, become the primary operational constraints as context windows expand.

4 Speculative Decoding: The target distribution is preserved by a modified rejection-sampling rule that accepts each drafted token with exactly the probability needed for the accepted sequence to follow the target model’s distribution (Leviathan et al. 2023; Chen et al. 2023). The guarantee is distributional, not bitwise. Speculative decoding reduces accelerator passes per generated token without changing which candidates can appear, so it leaves the reproducibility questions of section 2.4.3 unchanged.

Leviathan, Yaniv, Matan Kalman, and Yossi Matias. 2023. “Fast Inference from Transformers via Speculative Decoding.” Proceedings of the 40th International Conference on Machine Learning 202: 19274–86.
Chen, C., S. Borgeaud, G. Irving, J.-B. Lespiau, L. Sifre, and J. Jumper. 2023. “Accelerating Large Language Model Decoding with Speculative Sampling.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2302.01318.

Because each generated token extends a dependent sequence, output length is a major cost variable of a call, and the serving implementation sets its realized latency. Although the conditional dependence in equation 2 fixes the mathematical order of token choices, it does not mandate that hardware evaluate each token in an isolated forward pass. Techniques such as speculative decoding,4 for example, draft candidate token blocks with a small auxiliary model and score them in parallel with the target model, provably preserving the target output distribution (Leviathan et al. 2023; Chen et al. 2023). The per-token decode floor derived in section 2.8 is therefore a property of plain one-token-per-pass decoding, not a law of the factorization.

2.4.2 Sampling controls

Lowering sampling randomness can make a model repeat its top-scoring token choice, yet the highest-scoring token is often a plausible guess rather than a valid file path or a working patch. For positive temperature5 \(\SamplingTemperature\) (subscripted to keep it distinct from the trajectory \(\Trajectory\)), softmax converts the logits into next-token probabilities:

5 Temperature: The term comes from statistical mechanics, where the Boltzmann distribution weights a state of energy \(\BoltzmannStateEnergy\) by \(\exp(-\BoltzmannStateEnergy/\BoltzmannConstant \BoltzmannTemperature)\); reading each negative logit as an energy makes equation 3 identical in form. In a physical system, high temperature spreads particles across many states, whereas low temperature concentrates them near the ground state. In language model decoding, scaling temperature trades candidate diversity against concentration on the mode without changing which token scores highest.

\[\Pr(\OutputToken_t = \VocabularyToken \mid \InputTokenSequence, \OutputTokenPrefix) = \frac{\exp\left(\OutputLogit_{t, \VocabularyToken} / \SamplingTemperature\right)}{\sum_{\NormalizerTokenIndex \in \TokenVocabulary} \exp\left(\OutputLogit_{t, \NormalizerTokenIndex} / \SamplingTemperature\right)} \tag{3}\]

Unit temperature (\(\SamplingTemperature = 1.0\)) leaves the logits unscaled. Lowering a positive temperature amplifies logit differences and concentrates probability mass on the highest-scoring tokens; raising it flattens the distribution, which approaches uniformity across a finite vocabulary as temperature grows without bound. Temperature therefore controls output entropy, not model confidence: because next-token cross-entropy training minimizes sequence prediction error rather than calibrating scores to task success, high token probability does not mean a completed response is correct.

Greedy decoding instead picks a highest-scoring token, \(\operatorname{argmax}_{\VocabularyToken \in \TokenVocabulary} \OutputLogit_{t, \VocabularyToken}\), with a tie-breaking rule. Given fixed logits and a fixed tie rule, that choice is deterministic. Serving APIs often expose greedy decoding as \(\SamplingTemperature = 0\), which the engine implements by taking the argmax directly rather than dividing by zero in equation 3. As positive temperature approaches zero, softmax puts all probability mass on the maximum, though if several tokens tie for the top logit, the limit alone does not pick among them.

Sampling can also restrict which tokens remain eligible. Top-\(\TopTokenSampleCount\) sampling (Fan et al. 2018) keeps the \(\TopTokenSampleCount\) highest-scoring tokens under a tie rule and renormalizes their probabilities. Top-\(\NucleusProbabilityMass\) sampling, or nucleus sampling, sorts tokens by descending probability, keeps the shortest prefix whose cumulative mass reaches \(\NucleusProbabilityMass\) (Holtzman et al. 2020), and renormalizes that set. Because top-\(\TopTokenSampleCount\) fixes the candidate count regardless of shape, it can keep unlikely tail tokens when the distribution is sharp and cut plausible options when the distribution is flat. Top-\(\NucleusProbabilityMass\) instead lets the active pool expand or shrink with the distribution’s spread. When temperature scaling and nucleus truncation are used together, the order in which they run changes the active pool: scaling logits by \(\SamplingTemperature\) before computing probabilities alters how much mass the leading tokens carry and therefore changes how many tokens are needed to reach the cumulative threshold \(\NucleusProbabilityMass\). That ordering and the engine’s numerical conventions are therefore part of the decoding configuration.

Neither filter tells valid file paths, true claims, or safe commands from wrong ones. A rare token may be the exact identifier a patch needs, while a high-probability continuation may name a file that does not exist. Truncation filters change which candidates reach the runtime; only checks against the environment and task contract tell whether that change helps.

Nucleus truncation keeps the two tokens whose mass reaches 0.90 and zeroes the gray tail.

Napkin Math 2.1: Temperature and truncation on the same logits
Problem: A hypothetical vocabulary \(\TokenVocabulary = \{\VocabularyToken_1, \VocabularyToken_2, \VocabularyToken_3, \VocabularyToken_4, \VocabularyToken_5\}\) has logits \(\LogitVector_t = [12, 10.5, 9, 6, 3]\), held fixed so that differences arise strictly from the decoding configuration. How much probability mass concentrates onto the top token when halving the temperature, and which tokens survive nucleus truncation?

Math: At temperature \(\SamplingTemperature =\) 1, the exponentiated logits sum to 207596.9. Normalizing each exponential by this partition sum yields the baseline distribution:

\(\UnitTemperatureProbabilityVector \approx [0.7840, 0.1749, 0.0390, 0.0019, 0.0001]\).

Halving the temperature to \(\SamplingTemperature =\) 0.5 doubles the exponent of each term. Following normalization, the probability of the mode \(\VocabularyToken_1\) rises to 0.9503, capturing 95.0 percent of the total distribution. All lower-ranked tokens retain positive probability under untruncated softmax.

Returning to unit temperature and applying nucleus truncation with \(\NucleusProbabilityMass =\) 0.90 evaluates cumulative probability mass. The lead token \(\VocabularyToken_1\) provides 0.7840, which falls short of the threshold. Incorporating \(\VocabularyToken_2\) (0.1749) brings cumulative coverage to 0.9589, which satisfies the threshold and defines the active nucleus as \(\{\VocabularyToken_1, \VocabularyToken_2\}\). Renormalizing across this active set assigns probability 0.8176 to \(\VocabularyToken_1\) and 0.1824 to \(\VocabularyToken_2\), while strictly zeroing the remaining tail tokens \(\{\VocabularyToken_3, \VocabularyToken_4, \VocabularyToken_5\}\).

Systems insight: Temperature governs candidate diversity. Halving temperature compresses the non-top probability tail from 21.6 percent to 5.0 percent, achieving a 4.3× reduction, whereas nucleus truncation eliminates the tail entirely. Repeated calls produce diverse outputs only when probability mass occupies this tail, which is the stochastic resource that parallel candidate sampling draws on in Parallel sampling along the breadth axis. Because neither transformation ensures that the highest-scoring candidate is correct, engineering teams configure decoding parameters based on empirical task pass rates rather than raw model likelihood.

2.4.3 Repetition and replay

Greedy decoding removes sampling variance from a fixed logit vector, but it does not make served API calls reproducible end to end. In a production cluster, two identical requests can land on different server nodes, accelerator revisions, or kernel builds. Small differences in floating-point reduction order6 can shift nearly tied logits in their least significant bits, and flipping an argmax choice at position \(\TokenIndex=1\) changes the prefix for every token after it. Fixing a pseudorandom seed controls the sampler’s random number generator, but it cannot prevent numerical differences across backend kernels or dynamic batch sizes. Reproducing a call from scratch requires pinning the serving environment and kernels as well as setting \(\SamplingTemperature = 0\). A 2025 production incident shows how serving-fleet bugs alone can change model outputs while caller requests stay identical.

6 Floating-Point Reduction Order: Floating-point addition is not associative because each intermediate sum rounds (Goldberg 1991). Parallel attention and reduction kernels that sum dot products across different thread-block layouts or dynamic batch sizes add numbers in different orders, so the same request can produce logits that differ in their low bits depending on which other requests share its batch.

Goldberg, David. 1991. “What Every Computer Scientist Should Know about Floating-Point Arithmetic.” ACM Computing Surveys 23 (1): 5–48. https://doi.org/10.1145/103162.103163.

War Story 2.1: Three serving bugs behind unchanged requests (2025)
Context: Between August and early September 2025, three separate infrastructure bugs intermittently degraded the responses of Anthropic’s Claude models (Anthropic 2025). These defects stemmed entirely from the serving infrastructure rather than caller requests or model weights.

Mechanism: A front-end routing defect directed standard traffic to server clusters configured for extended context windows, while session stickiness (routing a client’s follow-up requests to the server that handled its earlier ones) kept those follow-up requests on the misconfigured nodes. Concurrently, an improperly deployed kernel optimization on a specialized accelerator cluster occasionally assigned outsized probability to improbable tokens. Finally, an optimization in the token-selection pipeline triggered a compiler bug: an approximate top-\(\TopTokenSampleCount\) routine with mixed-precision subroutines emitted incorrect tokens under specific dynamic batch sizes.

Impact: During peak impact, the routing failure corrupted 16 percent of requests to one production model, and approximately 30 percent of active coding agent users encountered at least one misrouted interaction. Generated responses contained foreign-language script insertions within English prose and syntax anomalies in generated source code. Automated regression suites initially missed these failures because a response often recovered from an isolated corrupted token later in generation, and intermittent runtime conditions meant an identical prompt could succeed on one invocation and fail on the next.

Fix: Engineers rolled back the offending kernel deployments, corrected cluster routing rules, and replaced the approximate top-\(\TopTokenSampleCount\) kernel with an exact selection routine running in 32-bit floating-point precision, trading a minor throughput penalty for numerical stability. Anthropic subsequently deployed continuous canary evaluations across production fleets to catch subtle generational drift.

Systems lesson: Output distributions reflect the concrete serving pipeline just as heavily as the model weights or input context. Corrupted tokens arrived packaged in well-formed HTTP payloads and cleanly closed generation blocks, a direct instance of the fail-plausible fault (The fail-plausible fault). Agent runtimes must log full call-level metadata (runtime versions, routing tags, and sampling parameters) while subjecting every proposed action to external environment validation regardless of nominal generation success.

Anthropic. 2025. A Postmortem of Three Recent Issues. Anthropic engineering incident report.

Incidents like this one set the logging baseline for an agent runtime. The runtime log should record the serialized prompt, model and tokenizer identifiers, sampling settings, token limits, and any routing or seed metadata, alongside the raw response payload, stop reason, and token usage. Those records also separate two kinds of debugging: replaying a recorded response tests the runtime’s downstream logic without model variance, whereas re-sending the live request tests how the agent handles variation across calls. Benchmarks should measure and report that call-to-call variance rather than judging a configuration from a single run.

An agent runtime therefore receives candidate tokens produced under a specific decoding configuration and ended by a specific stop condition. That stop condition is the first thing the runtime must read. A generation that ran into its output ceiling, was refused, or was cut off in transit can still deliver bytes that look like a finished proposal, and no check on the payload alone tells the difference. Before asking whether a read_file proposal names an existing, permitted path, the runtime needs a record of whether a complete candidate arrived at all.

2.5 The Invocation Contract

When a repair call hits its output token limit after emitting read_file("src/pool") instead of read_file("src/pool.py"), or when a local deadline expires while the remote server keeps decoding and billing tokens, raw reply text cannot tell the runtime what happened. Both strings parse as valid arguments, and an empty socket says nothing about remote execution. To keep how a call ended attached to what it returned, the host runtime pairs each outbound request specification with a normalized inbound response envelope so that every remote generation is governed by a single contract.

Definition 2.2: Model invocation contract

Model invocation contract is the pair of records that bounds one model call: a request fixing the input, model configuration, sampling settings, and enforced output and time limits, and a normalized envelope reporting the call’s status, payload, and resource use.

  1. Significance: The contract establishes each model call as a distinct, countable unit within an agent trajectory. Its usage record prices the call’s share of \(\AttemptCost\), and its status flag isolates completed generations from partial or failed calls. Consequently, the cost per accepted task (equation 1.2) can be computed from recorded telemetry rather than heuristically reconstructed from conversation transcripts.
  2. Distinction: The invocation contract describes the model call rather than the overarching task. A generated tool request can fully conform to decoding limits without ever being dispatched, and a fully generated response can fail downstream validation. Dispatch authorization, tool execution, and task-level acceptance remain separate runtime decisions backed by their own evidence.
  3. Common pitfall: Recording only the payload text. Without explicit status flags and usage accounting, a truncated fragment is indistinguishable from an intentional short response, and unrecorded token consumption silently distorts operational cost accounting as zero-cost execution.

2.5.1 What a request specifies

Leaving any part of a model request implicit, such as an unpinned model alias that silently updates, an unenforced token cap, or an unrecorded sampling setting, makes it impossible to price the call or explain why its behavior changed. At the generation interface, the bounded request fixes six fields:

\[\ModelCallRequest = \langle \RequestPromptTokens, \text{ModelID}, \OutputLimit, \CompletionDeadline, \StopSequenceSet, \SamplingParameters \rangle\]

The first field, \(\RequestPromptTokens = (\PromptToken_1, \PromptToken_2, \dots, \PromptToken_{\SequenceLength})\), encodes the serialized messages, role markers, advertised tool schemas, and formatting delimiters visible to the model, which the runtime builds from structured messages and tools inputs before serialization. Once the input sequence is fixed, ModelID and \(\SamplingParameters\) specify how the serving stack evaluates it. ModelID names the model weights and serving deployment selected through model_config, where engineers distinguish mutable provider aliases (such as model-latest) from pinned, immutable revisions (such as dated or content-addressed snapshots). The sampling parameters \(\SamplingParameters\), including temperature and top-\(\NucleusProbabilityMass\), then fix the selection rule used during decoding. Together with the serialized input and returned output, those fields document the call; exact regeneration also depends on the serving path, as war story 2.1 showed.

To bound how long that generation may run, the request pairs an output allowance \(\OutputLimit\) with a local waiting deadline \(\CompletionDeadline\). Because a system cannot rely on the model to stop on time, invariant closure (principle 1) places both limits below the model, where the host runtime and serving system enforce them mechanically. Before sending the call, the runtime checks that the prompt and output allowance fit inside the context window, and the adapter notes whether the provider bills hidden reasoning tokens against \(\OutputLimit\), since counting only visible text would understate what the call spent.

Generation can also end before reaching \(\OutputLimit\) when the output matches one of the stop sequences in \(\StopSequenceSet\), which halt decoding as soon as the serving engine encounters them. Because the engine matches raw byte or character sequences without parsing structure, a stop sequence that appears inside an argument or quoted string can cut off generation before the closing quotes or braces are emitted. The runtime must therefore record the provider’s stop reason and validate syntax separately.

Resource limits are enforced in two places: on the client host and on the remote serving cluster. Locally, the host runtime caps incoming response bytes and stops waiting when the deadline \(\CompletionDeadline\) expires. A local timeout ends the caller’s wait and marks the call failed, but it does not prove that the remote server stopped decoding or freed accelerator memory.7 Server-side token caps and cancellation protocols handle the remote side, so every limit should state which component enforces it and which resource it bounds.

7 Remote Call Semantics: Remote procedure call designers met this ambiguity first. In the design Birrell and Nelson (1984) describe, a call that returns normally executed exactly once, but a call that ends in a failure report may have executed once or not at all, because the caller cannot tell a lost request from a lost reply. A model call inherits the same gap: a timed-out generation may still have run to completion and been billed, which is why the normalized status for a failed delivery, TRANSPORT_FAILURE (table 2), records an unknown remote outcome rather than a call that never happened.

Birrell, Andrew D., and Bruce Jay Nelson. 1984. “Implementing Remote Procedure Calls.” ACM Transactions on Computer Systems 2 (1): 39–59. https://doi.org/10.1145/2080.357392.

2.5.2 The normalized status envelope

If a runtime inspects only the returned text or tool arguments, a partial string cut off by a token ceiling looks just like a finished proposal. To keep how a call ended attached to what it returned, the runtime wraps every response in a normalized status envelope:

\[\NormalizedStatusEnvelope = \langle \text{status}, \ResponsePayload, \ResponseMetadata \rangle\]

Here, \(\ResponsePayload\) is the returned payload, which may be complete, partial, or empty, and \(\ResponseMetadata\) holds the call’s telemetry: the response kind, the raw provider stop_reason, reported token usage, request identifiers, latency timestamps, and serving settings. Keeping \(\ResponsePayload\) paired with \(\ResponseMetadata\) prevents downstream code from reading the payload without its status, and records unreported usage as unknown rather than zero.

Because different model APIs report stop reasons, error codes, and transport failures in incompatible ways, the runtime adapter maps provider-specific signals onto the seven normalized statuses in table 2. Two of them, REJECTED and CANCELED, occur only while a response is streaming (section 2.5.3). Each status records what the host runtime verified locally; if two events race, such as a local cancellation crossing an incoming completion frame, the adapter resolves the status deterministically while keeping the raw provider signals in \(\ResponseMetadata\).

For a read_file request, kind="tool_request" tells the runtime which schema to check, while status="COMPLETED" confirms that generation finished normally. Neither field says that the tool ran or that the file read succeeded; completion describes generation, whereas tool execution and task acceptance are separate runtime decisions.

Applying invariant closure (principle 1) to the status field gives a hard gate: the quarantining rule. Under this rule, no payload whose status is anything other than COMPLETED may enter the candidate validation or tool-dispatch path. The runtime checks status before parsing or dispatching the payload. It can log an interrupted or malformed fragment for diagnosis, but that log entry cannot trigger a tool call or be promoted to a finished proposal. When later chapters sample several candidates for one task, any candidate cut off by a token limit obeys the same rule: a truncated payload is a diagnosable failure, never a partial action.

A destructive shell command shows why parsing alone cannot replace this status check. Suppose the complete intended command is

rm -rf /tmp/scratch/cache_dir

If an exhausted token quota cuts off generation mid-path, the prefix is

rm -rf /tmp/scratch

That truncated prefix is valid shell syntax, yet it deletes the entire parent directory. Because the envelope marks the call TRUNCATED, the quarantining rule blocks the command before it reaches the shell. Conversely, status="COMPLETED" grants no permission to run; it establishes only that the model finished emitting its proposal. Listing 3 implements this admission check, passing only completed generations to the candidate path while leaving syntax, permissions, and task verification to later stages.

Incomplete payloads go to a diagnostic log with its own storage and access limits, keeping the partial byte stream and stop_reason for debugging. “Repairing” a truncated fragment by appending guessed arguments or closing braces invents a new candidate that the model never completed, and such patched fragments must never be marked COMPLETED.

2.5.3 Streaming and cancellation

Waiting for a four-thousand-token response to finish before checking its structure wastes seconds of accelerator time when the first key violates the tool schema. Streaming delivers response fragments over the connection as generation proceeds, letting the runtime inspect prefixes early, provided its parser follows the provider’s framing protocol rather than assuming each network chunk holds one token. When an arriving prefix violates the required format, the stream monitor stops waiting immediately instead of buffering the rest of an unusable reply. Because generation is still in flight, the monitor checks whether the prefix can still be completed into a valid structure, not whether it is already complete.

Rejection and cancellation are distinct events. When the stream monitor spots a structural violation, the runtime sets the status to REJECTED and sends a cancel request to the service. When an operator or runtime policy aborts a compliant stream before completion, the runtime marks the status CANCELED. A local deadline that expires before completion maps instead to TRANSPORT_FAILURE (table 2), after which the runtime may also send a cancel request. Keeping the partial payload out of tool dispatch is a local guarantee the host enforces in all three cases. Stopping the remote decode loop and freeing accelerator memory, however, depends on the serving engine honoring the cancel signal; merely closing a client socket does not prove that remote decoding stopped.

Consider a call with an output allowance of 4,096 tokens whose stream validator detects a schema violation at step \(\RejectionDecodeStep =\) 96. The runtime’s part of the response finishes at once. It records the rejection, discards the partial proposal, and sends a cancel request. The service’s part depends on whether that request is honored (figure 3).

The skipped-step count shown is an upper bound that requires provider cooperation. If the serving engine processes the cancellation promptly, it skips up to \(\OutputLimit - \RejectionDecodeStep =\) 4,000 autoregressive decode steps. In the reference unbatched deployment, each plain autoregressive decode step reads the model weights from accelerator memory, so the step takes at least the weight bytes divided by the memory bandwidth; for the reference deployment of section 2.8, that floor is 21.075 ms per generated token. At that reference floor, skipping all these steps avoids 84.3 s of estimated decode work. An honored cancellation also frees the request’s slot among the requests the serving engine decodes together and releases the call’s KV-cache allocations. If the remote service fails to honor the cancellation, the host runtime still quarantines the rejected payload locally, but the remote service may continue generating up to its token allowance.

Nonstreaming calls apply the same status gate once the whole response arrives. Streaming moves the check earlier in the call so the runtime can reject bad prefixes and reclaim resources sooner, without relaxing the rule that incomplete prefixes never run. Either way, the status gate settles only whether a complete candidate arrived. A COMPLETED read_file proposal can still name a missing file or a path outside the workspace, so the next question is what each subsequent check on that candidate actually proves.

2.6 Checking Candidate Outputs

Passing the status gate admits a completed proposal to the candidate path, whether that proposal is the initial read_file("src/pool.py") request or the connection-pool patch the repair agent emits on the next turn. Neither high token probability nor schema conformance determines whether the runtime can safely act on either candidate: a well-formed read_file request can still target a missing or unauthorized path (section 2.2.2), and a candidate patch can parse cleanly, compile against existing headers, and pass a sequential unit test while leaving the underlying race condition untouched. Because each verification check answers a narrow question rather than certifying overall task satisfaction, the runtime must track what each gate actually proves and what it leaves open.

2.6.1 Why token likelihood cannot replace checks

A model often assigns high probability to an obsolete or nonexistent file path because that path matches common directory naming patterns in its training corpus. Autoregressive generation scores tokens by how well they fit training data and prompt context, not by whether a path exists on disk, whether the caller has permission to read it, or whether an edited function preserves a lock invariant.

Checking a candidate therefore means pairing each required property with the tool or gate that actually tests it. For a read_file proposal, a schema validator checks that the payload has only allowed keys and a string-valued path. Passing that check proves schema conformance, provided the validator implements the schema dialect faithfully and inspects the exact object passed to execution. Proving that the target file exists requires checking the filesystem, and proving permission requires checking the task’s capability grant against the host policy.

Static checks and dynamic tests likewise establish different claims. A compiler checks types and syntax under fixed flags, while a linter flags suspect patterns with static rules. Dynamic tests run concrete code paths and check runtime assertions, so a passing single-threaded checkout test says nothing about how the connection pool behaves under concurrent contention.

↩︎ The closure evidence levels, from self-report and static checks through visible and sealed tests to formal proof, are established in The invariant closure principle.

8 Complete Mediation: One of the design principles that Saltzer and Schroeder (1975) set out for protection systems: every access to every object is checked against current authority at the moment of access, not remembered from an earlier decision. The tool handler applies the same rule to a model’s proposal, so a path validated when the request was composed is checked again when the read executes, closing the gap in which the grant or the file could have changed.

Saltzer, J. H., and M. D. Schroeder. 1975. “The Protection of Information in Computer Systems.” Proceedings of the IEEE 63 (9): 1278–308. https://doi.org/10.1109/proc.1975.9939.

Because each check covers only its own scope, the runtime must treat model output as untrusted data rather than executable control flow. A candidate tool call stays passive data until an authorized tool handler validates and dispatches it, checking permissions at the moment of access8 regardless of what the model claims about its own rights. Running an untrusted code patch likewise executes arbitrary machine instructions and belongs inside an isolated sandbox even during early test runs.

2.6.2 Self-review and external evidence

Asking a model to review or refine its own patch can catch surface syntax mistakes or missing edge cases, yet self-review is just another generation step drawn from the same weights and conditioned on the same incomplete context. When the model starts from a mistaken assumption about an API or lock protocol, prompting it to critique its own answer usually repeats that assumption rather than catching it. Re-prompting does not create an independent check: across reasoning benchmarks, models asked to grade and revise their own answers without new external evidence rarely improve accuracy and often turn right answers into wrong ones (Huang et al. 2024).

Huang, Jie, Xinyun Chen, Swaroop Mishra, Huaixiu Steven Zheng, Adams Wei Yu, Xinying Song, and Denny Zhou. 2024. “Large Language Models Cannot Self-Correct Reasoning Yet.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Zheng, Lianmin, Wei-Lin Chiang, Ying Sheng, Siyuan Zhuang, Hao Wu, Yonghao Zhuang, Zi Lin, et al. 2023. “Judging LLM-as-a-Judge with MT-Bench and Chatbot Arena.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 46595–623.

Refining a candidate and certifying a property must therefore stay separate. A model’s critique can guide the next search step, but external tools (compilers, linters, sandboxed test runners, and kernel permission checks) must supply the evidence for the properties they test. No model critique, whether from the generator itself or from a separate learned judge (a second model prompted or trained to score the first model’s output), carries authority to clear a security boundary or approve a release. When a pipeline uses a learned judge to rank or filter candidates, the runtime should treat its verdicts as noisy scores with measurable false-accept rates and known biases, such as favoring longer answers or particular list positions (Zheng et al. 2023). Even when a check returns a passing verdict, a conditional-probability calculation shows how much defect risk remains when the checker itself can pass a defective patch.

Systems Perspective 2.1: What a passing verdict leaves unresolved
Let \(\CandidateDefectRate\) denote the defect rate, the share of generated patches that violate a specified behavioral property (a local symbol here, distinct from the per-turn error rate of horizon compounding), and let \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\) denote the checker’s false-accept rate, the probability that it passes a defective patch. Suppose \(\CandidateDefectRate =\) 18 percent, \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate =\) 0.65, and the checker passes valid patches with probability 0.85. Bayes’ rule gives the fraction of defective patches among the passing results:

\[\Pr(\text{Defective} \mid \text{PASS}) = \frac{\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate \cdot \CandidateDefectRate}{\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate \cdot \CandidateDefectRate + \Pr(\text{PASS} \mid \text{Valid}) \cdot (1 - \CandidateDefectRate)} \tag{4}\]

Substituting the assumed rates gives \(\frac{0.65 \times 0.18}{(0.65 \times 0.18) + (0.85 \times (1 - 0.18))}\approx 14.37\%\). The defect share among passing patches falls only from 18 percent to 14.37 percent, because the checker rejects 35 percent of defective patches while also rejecting 15 percent of valid ones. The lever is \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\): a checker with half the false-accept rate, 0.325, lowers the residual to 7.7 percent. Repeating the same check buys no such guarantee, because its behavior on the surviving candidates is correlated with the first pass. Every defective patch that passes also inflates the apparent acceptance probability \(\AcceptanceProbability\) in the cost per accepted task (equation 1.2).

A sound checker for the exact property drives \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\) to zero under its assumptions. Determinism alone does not supply soundness, and soundness for schema membership does not extend to patch behavior. The residual in equation 4 is one minus the precision \(\VerifierPrecision\) of a passing verdict in the verification asymmetry (principle 3), written there with valid-candidate rate \(\ValidCandidateRate = 1 - \CandidateDefectRate\) and false-reject rate \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate = 1 - \Pr(\text{PASS} \mid \text{Valid})\). That principle holds that producing a candidate, selecting it, and establishing that it satisfies the task are different events, and it yields a design rule. Select each validation gate for the specific property it establishes and its false-accept rate, and let the task contract determine what residual defect rate the system can tolerate.

2.6.3 Staging checks by cost and scope

Running full end-to-end integration tests or sandboxed reproduction scripts on every model output is orders of magnitude more expensive than parsing JSON or running a static linter. The runtime therefore stages candidate checks from cheapest to most expensive, rejecting malformed or unauthorized requests early before spending compute on behavioral tests. Ordering checks by cost improves throughput, yet each gate tests a different property rather than a tighter approximation of overall correctness. Table 3 lists these four layers along with what each costs and what its passing result leaves open.

Consider how this pipeline handles an inspection request versus a patch. Behind the status gate of section 2.5, a read_file proposal needs only the first two rows of table 3 before dispatch, because reading the file supplies an observation for the next turn rather than finishing the repair. When evaluating a proposed patch, by contrast, the pipeline checks patch syntax and target paths, applies the diff in an isolated scratch workspace, and runs the required unit and stress tests. Final acceptance follows the task contract’s evidence standard, escalating to human review when automated checks cannot settle safety or correctness.

Every layer in table 3 inspects tokens the model has already emitted. Even when a streaming monitor aborts a broken response early, the offending token has already been chosen, and recovering still requires another call. For the first layer of that cascade, syntax and schema conformance, the runtime knows the legal structure before decoding begins and can enforce it inside the sampler itself.

2.7 Grammar-Guided Decoding

If the repair agent’s read_file("src/pool.py") call omits the closing quotation mark before emitting an end-of-turn token, the schema validator in table 3 catches the malformed JSON and blocks dispatch, but recovering still costs another full round of queueing, prefill, and decoding. Because the request already declares the tool schema ahead of time, the inference engine does not need to wait for an illegal token to arrive at the parser; it can mask out every vocabulary token that would violate the required syntax at each decode step, so a syntax error cannot be sampled in the first place.

Definition 2.3: Grammar-constrained decoding

Grammar-constrained decoding restricts next-token selection during autoregressive generation to tokens whose decoded text preserves a viable prefix of a target formal grammar.

  1. Significance: Dynamic token masking guarantees that any completed generation sequence strictly satisfies the structural grammar, eliminating syntax errors at the model boundary when the constraint parser covers the target schema.
  2. Distinction: Post-generation validation inspects an already emitted sequence and discards invalid payloads after full generation. Constrained decoding actively shapes the vocabulary distribution at every generation step. Even so, neither approach verifies whether a syntactically correct payload contains sensible parameter values or possesses valid execution permissions.
  3. Common pitfall: Mistaking a viable prefix for a completed data structure. If token limits, request timeouts, or connection failures truncate generation early, the emitted sequence remains an unfinished prefix that cannot be safely dispatched.

2.7.1 Legal prefixes and completed structures

If a model emits an unquoted identifier or a stray comma after {"path":, the entire JSON payload breaks and no later token can repair it. To block illegal tokens before they are sampled, the constraint engine keeps a running parse state synchronized with the sequence generated so far. For a schema expecting a string-valued path parameter, once {"path": appears in the buffer, the grammar allows optional whitespace followed by an opening quotation mark, and forbids unquoted booleans, numbers, or bare identifiers. When the generator emits {"path":"src/, the text is a viable prefix:9 although incomplete as a JSON document, it can still be completed into a valid object by later string and delimiter tokens. Constrained decoding admits a candidate token only if it can extend the current prefix toward an accepting grammar state.

9 Legal (Viable) Prefix: The viable-prefix concept comes from classical compiler theory. The LR parsers introduced by Knuth (1965) read input strings left to right and report an error at the first symbol that cannot extend the prefix into any valid sentence of the language. A grammar mask applies the same property constructively, pruning invalid tokens from the vocabulary before sampling instead of catching syntax errors after generation finishes.

Knuth, Donald E. 1965. “On the Translation of Languages from Left to Right.” Information and Control 8 (6): 607–39. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0019-9958(65)90426-2.

At generation step \(\TokenIndex\), let \(\DecodingConstraintState_t\) represent the parser’s internal state, including nested scope tracking for recursive structures. Let \(\ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t)\) denote the set of vocabulary tokens whose decoded strings maintain a viable prefix under the target grammar. Because modern language models operate on subword tokens rather than individual characters, a single token can span multiple characters, punctuation marks, or whitespace segments (for example, a single token .py"} that finishes the extension, closes the string, and closes the object). The engine must therefore simulate the parser across the token’s full decoded byte sequence to confirm that every character transition is valid.

For each token index \(\MaskTokenIndex\) in vocabulary \(\TokenVocabulary\), the constraint engine constructs an additive logit mask:

\[\LogitMaskEntry_i(\DecodingConstraintState_t) = \begin{cases} 0 & \text{if } \MaskTokenIndex \in \ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t) \\ -\infty & \text{if } \MaskTokenIndex \notin \ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t) \end{cases}\]

Adding this mask to the unnormalized logit vector \(\LogitVector_t\) produces the filtered logit vector \(\MaskedLogitVector_t = \LogitVector_t + \LogitMask(\DecodingConstraintState_t)\). At unit temperature, the resulting softmax distribution becomes

\[\SoftmaxOutputProbability_{t, i} = \frac{\exp(\MaskedTokenLogit_{t, i})}{\sum_{j \in \TokenVocabulary} \exp(\MaskedTokenLogit_{t, j})} = \begin{cases} \frac{\exp(\OutputLogit_{t, i})}{\sum_{k \in \ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t)} \exp(\OutputLogit_{t, k})} & \text{if } \MaskTokenIndex \in \ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t) \\ 0 & \text{if } \MaskTokenIndex \notin \ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t) \end{cases} \tag{5}\]

Setting the logits of invalid tokens to \(-\infty\) forces their sampling probabilities to zero, renormalizing the distribution entirely across viable continuations. Once the sampler draws a token from this restricted set, the engine consumes that token’s characters and advances the parser to state \(\DecodingConstraintState_{t+1}\). This formulation assumes that \(\ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t)\) remains nonempty and that the sampling configuration respects the mask. The engine applies the mask before top-\(\TopTokenSampleCount\) or nucleus truncation so that these filters select among legal continuations. If an implementation instead truncates first and no retained token belongs to \(\ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t)\), the serving runtime must raise an explicit constraint-satisfaction error rather than silently disabling the mask. Positive finite temperature rescales the remaining probabilities without removing their support.

Recomputing \(\ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t)\) from scratch at every step would cost a scan of the whole vocabulary per token. For a regular grammar or a flat tool schema with a finite state space, engines compile the schema once into a finite-state automaton whose states each carry a precomputed mask \(\LogitMask(\DecodingConstraintState_t)\), so that each step is a table lookup followed by a state transition (Willard and Louf 2023). When a grammar allows recursive nesting, such as nested JSON objects or parenthesized expressions, a pushdown stack tracks the open scopes at runtime while precomputed masks still accelerate the lexical transitions at each state. Incremental parsing of this kind yields syntactically valid output even for a language as structured as SQL (Scholak et al. 2021). The automaton in figure 4 is compiled for a query tool with two fields, query and limit, rather than for read_file, and the masking mechanism is identical.

The resulting guarantee is conditional. The mask keeps every prefix viable, and the engine disallows the end-of-sequence (<eos>) token while required fields or closing delimiters remain unwritten, so an output that ends normally belongs to the enforced language. Nothing in the mask makes generation end normally within its token budget.

↪ The automaton behind the token mask, and the induction showing that a constrained output ending in a permitted end token belongs to the grammar, are developed in Finite-state constrained decoding.

Consider what happens when an inference request hits its maximum output token limit while emitting arguments, leaving the decoding buffer at {"path":"src/po. The string is a viable prefix under the schema, yet the envelope reports status: TRUNCATED, and the quarantining rule of section 2.5.2 applies. A heuristic repair parser that appends the closing quote and brace would fabricate the call read_file("src/po"), which the model never finished and which names a file nobody asked for. The mask governs which tokens can appear; it never promotes an interrupted generation to a completed one.

2.7.2 What a grammar mask guarantees

Grammar masking moves the first row of table 3 into generation and leaves every other row where it was. A masked {"path":"src/pool.py"} still names a file that may not exist or may lie outside the grant, because the mask operates strictly on the textual representation and never queries repository state or security policy. Property by property, the mask’s own reach is narrow (table 4).

How much a grammar mask guarantees depends on the grammar it compiles. Enforcing a general JSON grammar guarantees only RFC 8259 syntax (balanced braces, legal numbers, and escaped strings) without restricting keys or value types. Compiling a specific JSON Schema into the constraint engine goes further, enforcing required fields, distinguishing integers from floats, and restricting strings to declared regular expressions or enumerations.

Even a schema-aware mask has limits when a schema uses features that cannot be compiled into a finite-state or pushdown automaton. Keywords that enforce cross-field conditionals (if/then/else), unique array elements, or arbitrary lookahead often exceed what precomputed token masks support. Whenever an engine drops unsupported schema keywords, has a bug in its grammar compiler, or parses types differently from the downstream consumer, the guarantee stops holding. The runtime should therefore document which schema features its mask enforces and still run a final check against the authoritative schema before dispatch. Under invariant closure (principle 1), logit masking is a mechanical bound below the model: it enforces syntax without the model’s cooperation while leaving authorization and semantic checks above the model.

A strict schema can also force a model to guess when context is missing. If a schema requires a path string with no alternative branch, every completed output must supply a path even when the prompt gives no clue which file to inspect. Stripping out every token outside that single object shape leaves the model no way to report that information is missing, so it must invent a plausible path to reach an accepting parser state.

Adding an explicit unknown variant or a structured error branch to the schema avoids that forced guessing and lets the model report missing context cleanly. Those branches are semantic choices inside the payload, distinct from transport statuses and tool errors. They make an honest report possible without forcing one, since the model can still guess a path when unknown is available, or pick unknown when the answer is already in the prompt. The runtime therefore handles each branch under the task contract, gathering another observation or asking the user when the model reports uncertainty.

2.7.3 Learned formatting and runtime constraints

Fine-tuning a model on tool examples makes well-formed arguments likely but still permits rare syntax slips on long contexts, whereas a grammar mask alone forces valid syntax even when the model has no idea which tool or argument fits the task. The two mechanisms work at different layers and complement each other. Supervised fine-tuning (further training on curated example responses, developed in Trajectory Fine-Tuning) and instruction tuning teach models to map prompts onto tool definitions and put high probability on appropriate arguments (Schick et al. 2023). Runtime logit masking fixes the support of the distribution at each step by setting illegal tokens to zero probability. A model tuned for tool use puts high logits on sensible tokens, and the runtime mask guarantees that sampling noise or long-context drift cannot emit broken syntax. Neither replaces the other: training alone gives no hard guarantee against syntax errors, and a grammar mask supplies no domain knowledge about which file path is right.

Schick, Timo, Jane Dwivedi-Yu, Roberto Dessı̀, Roberta Raileanu, Maria Lomeli, Luke Zettlemoyer, Nicola Cancedda, and Thomas Scialom. 2023. “Toolformer: Language Models Can Teach Themselves to Use Tools.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 68539–51.

Grammar constraints also carry their own costs. Compiling complex regular expressions or nested JSON schemas into deterministic automata consumes CPU time and memory. In dynamic environments where agents construct ad hoc schemas per request, compilation latency can offset the latency saved by avoiding syntax retries. Similarly, tracking parse states and computing masks across large vocabularies (\(|\TokenVocabulary| \ge 128{,}000\) tokens) adds per-token decoding overhead unless supported by precomputed lookup indices or bitmasks applied on the accelerator. Eliminating syntax retries lowers total cost only when compilation, mask memory, and per-token mask work together cost less than the retries they replace.

Checkpoint 2.2: Evaluating schema-constrained decoding

A repair agent uses constrained decoding for file-reading proposals. Its runtime records response kind and invocation status separately.

Constrained decoding removes one class of syntax failure before dispatch, but a structurally valid invocation still needs enough budget to finish and external evidence to justify execution. Whether a mask pays for itself, and what any call costs at all, depends on how prompt processing and token generation consume time on the serving hardware.

2.8 The Cost of a Model Call

On the repair agent’s first turn, reading a long prompt of repository context and tool schemas takes less accelerator time than generating a short reply, yet across a multi-turn repair loop, resending that growing prompt history balloons into hundreds of thousands of input tokens. Counting a call as a single black-box unit, or summing input and output tokens into one unweighted total, hides which phase actually drives latency and cluster work. An invocation contract therefore budgets prompt reading and output generation separately, pricing both a single call’s latency and a multi-turn trajectory’s cumulative input load.

Input length and output length stress distinct subsystems in the inference serving stack. Prompt ingestion exposes many known token positions to parallel matrix computation across the accelerator’s execution units. Conversely, autoregressive token generation executes as a strictly sequential loop, where each new token depends on the hidden state of all preceding positions. Because hardware handles these two phases under different physical constraints, output length is the primary software lever for controlling generation latency. A long prompt, a short reply, or a congested service queue can, however, shift the latency bottleneck elsewhere.

2.8.1 Where a call spends its time
