A runtime that records only the total wall-clock time of a slow call cannot tell whether the delay came from a bloated prompt, a backed-up serving queue, or a long reply, yet each cause requires a different fix. To separate them, the runtime measures five non-overlapping phases. They run from the moment it prepares the outbound request to the moment the normalized response is ready for validation. The phases are preparation (context assembly, serialization, and network send), queueing (waiting in the service’s admission queue), prefill (reading the prompt tokens in a parallel forward pass), decode (generating output tokens step by step), and postprocessing (response transfer, deserialization, and schema checks):

\[\CallLatency = \CallPreparationTime + \RequestQueueTime + \PrefillTime + \DecodeInterval + \CallPostprocessingTime \tag{6}\]

Each term in equation 6 is an elapsed time in seconds, measured with timestamps rather than estimated backward from token counts; when network transfer or local preparation overlaps server execution, the spans track non-overlapping intervals so nothing is counted twice. The first generated token needs careful accounting so it is not charged to both prefill and decode. In a standard Transformer, the prefill pass reads the whole prompt and produces the logits from which the first output token is drawn. On the client side, the time from sending the request to receiving that first token is the time to first token (TTFT), and the average spacing between later tokens is the time per output token (TPOT). Serving systems track TTFT and TPOT as separate objectives because prefill and decode contend for the same accelerator compute and memory bandwidth; disaggregated serving systems go further and run prefill and decode on separate accelerator pools so neither stalls the other (Zhong et al. 2024).

Zhong, Yinmin, Shengyu Liu, Junda Chen, Jianbo Hu, Yibo Zhu, Xuanzhe Liu, Xin Jin, and Hao Zhang. 2024. “DistServe: Disaggregating Prefill and Decoding for Goodput-Optimized Large Language Model Serving.” 18th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI 24), July, 193–210.

When TTFT includes sampling and delivering the first token, the remaining decode interval covers only the next \(\OutputLength-1\) tokens in a completion of length \(\OutputLength\). For a streamed reply, adding client-measured TTFT, the \(\OutputLength-1\) inter-token intervals, and final deserialization gives the full call latency without double-counting the first token. Client-measured TTFT is larger than server prefill time because it also includes network transit and queueing, and because servers and networks often batch several streamed tokens into one network chunk, chunk arrival times on the client do not always match per-token step times on the accelerator.

The first-token boundary separates these two measurements in figure 5. The service produces the initial logits during prefill, but the client must still wait for sampling and delivery before its TTFT interval ends.

A client trace can therefore identify a long wait for the first token without establishing how much of that wait the accelerator spent on prefill. Diagnosing the delay requires queueing and execution boundaries from the service, rather than a finer sampling of chunk arrivals.

Capping max_output_tokens bounds how many tokens the model can emit, not how fast the call returns; a congested queue can burn the entire deadline before prefill even starts. Latency benchmarks must therefore record the model, prompt and output lengths, prefix-cache hit rate, and cluster load. Even when a single agent runs one turn at a time, the serving cluster rarely runs that call alone: continuous batching schedules requests at each decode step (Yu et al. 2022), interleaving the agent’s tokens with dozens of other requests.

2.8.2 Estimating per-call hardware work

Counting input and output tokens together hides why a two-hundred-token reply can take longer on the accelerator than an eight-thousand-token prompt. Input processing and output generation hit different hardware bottlenecks, so a baseline work model gives them separate per-token time floors. A dense Transformer with \(\Parameters\) parameters executes approximately \(2\Parameters\) floating-point operations for each token it processes, in prefill and decode alike. What differs is how often the weights must be read. Prefill reads each weight tensor from accelerator memory once per call and reuses it across all \(\SequenceLength\) prompt positions, so a long prompt spreads one weight read over thousands of tokens of arithmetic, and each prompt token costs about \(2\Parameters\) operations at the peak arithmetic rate \(\PeakRate\). Single-stream decode produces one token per step, so it reads every weight once per generated token. Each output token therefore moves the full weight payload of \(\Parameters\,\ElementStorageSize\) bytes (with \(\ElementStorageSize\) bytes per parameter) across a memory interface of bandwidth \(\Bandwidth\), and its arithmetic finishes long before those bytes arrive.

These two regimes are the two limbs of the roofline model (Williams et al. 2009), which bounds attainable throughput by the lesser of the peak arithmetic rate and the memory bandwidth multiplied by the work done per byte moved. Prefill does much work per byte and sits under the arithmetic limb; low-batch decode does little and sits under the bandwidth limb, a split that empirical evaluations of Transformer inference confirm (Pope et al. 2023). The two floors are

Williams, Samuel, Andrew Waterman, and David Patterson. 2009. “Roofline: An Insightful Visual Performance Model for Multicore Architectures.” Communications of the ACM 52 (4): 65–76. https://doi.org/10.1145/1498765.1498785.

\[\PerTokenPrefillTime \approx \frac{2\Parameters}{\PeakRate}, \qquad \PerTokenDecodeTime \ge \frac{\Parameters\,\ElementStorageSize}{\Bandwidth} \tag{7}\]

The lowercase symbols are per-token floors. The analytical budget uses the allowances \(\PrefillTime \approx \SequenceLength\,\PerTokenPrefillTime\) and \(\DecodeInterval \approx \OutputLength\,\PerTokenDecodeTime\). Dividing the two floors cancels the parameter count \(\Parameters\):

\[\frac{\PerTokenDecodeTime}{\PerTokenPrefillTime} \approx \frac{\PeakRate \, \ElementStorageSize}{2 \, \Bandwidth} \tag{8}\]

Consider a concrete reference deployment: a dense model with 70.6B parameters stored in 8-bit precision (\(\ElementStorageSize = 1\) byte) on a single accelerator with an 8-bit peak rate of 1,979 TFLOP/s and a memory bandwidth of 3.35 TB/s. Each token costs 141.2 GFLOP of arithmetic, and dividing by the peak rate gives a prefill floor of 71.35 μs per prompt token. Each decode step reads 70.6 GB of weights, and dividing by the bandwidth gives a decode floor of 21.075 ms per generated token. Under equation 8, decoding a single output token therefore takes the same hardware time as ingesting roughly 295 prompt tokens. This asymmetry is the content of output-bound latency (principle 2), under which each plain, unbatched decode step incurs a full weight read in this model, so output volume can dominate call latency. The ratio reflects weight-dominated, unbatched decode against peak-throughput prefill, not a measured cloud service rate or a commercial billing tier.

↪ Both execution floors and their operating boundaries are developed in Cost Asymmetry Between Input Prefill and Output Generation.

This analytical budget charges each prompt token once at the prefill floor and reserves one decode floor step for each generated token. The first output token receives that allowance even though prefill supplies its initial logits. The budget therefore includes an extra decode-floor step relative to the measured post-TTFT interval, which contains only subsequent generation steps.

The budget is an idealized hardware baseline rather than a substitute for wall-clock measurement: it omits network transit, queueing delay, attention quadratics, and runtime software overhead. It also assumes standard autoregressive generation without speculative decoding. In production, a serving system that batches concurrent client requests, distributes model weights across tensor-parallel accelerators (sharding individual weight matrices across multiple devices), or reuses cached attention state substantially alters the relationship between token count and elapsed time. The analytical estimate is therefore suited to comparing interface formats and output volumes, but certifying a production deadline requires measuring latency directly under realistic serving load.

Applying this baseline to the first repair turn shows how output tokens dominate. A prompt of 8,000 tokens contributes 0.571 s of prefill work, while generating a reply of 200 tokens is charged 4.215 s of decode work. The total call estimate is 4.786 s. Although output constitutes only 2.4 percent of the call’s total tokens, it accounts for 88.1 percent of the modeled hardware time. Pruning redundant output tokens therefore reduces overall latency far more effectively than removing an identical number of prompt tokens. At the same time, an agent benefits from a cheaper invocation only if the model still receives sufficient diagnostic context to generate a correct candidate; engineers must prune verbosity from the output format, not critical evidence from the prompt.

2.8.3 How multi-turn history drives up input cost

Even when individual turns produce concise outputs, a multi-turn trajectory can still generate a large prompt-ingestion workload. Consider an agent runtime that maintains an append-only execution log and re-transmits the full cumulative history on every invocation. If the initial turn contains \(\BasePromptLength\) prompt tokens and each subsequent turn appends \(\TurnAppendLength\) tokens of combined model output and tool observations, a trajectory spanning a horizon of \(\TurnHorizon\) turns submits:

\[\PromptTokensSent = \sum_{j=0}^{\TurnHorizon-1} \left(\BasePromptLength + \PriorTurnCount\,\TurnAppendLength\right) = \TurnHorizon \BasePromptLength + \TurnAppendLength \frac{\TurnHorizon(\TurnHorizon-1)}{2} \tag{9}\]

prompt tokens in total. The quadratic growth in equation 9 comes from resending the growing history on every turn rather than from an inherent need of multi-turn reasoning. Sliding-window context buffers, trimmed tool outputs, or turn summaries can reduce the tokens sent, and a stateful serving endpoint can hold the conversation on the server to avoid resending the bytes over the network even when attention still spans the full history.

Re-sent input grows with the square of the horizon; prefix reuse keeps fresh prefill linear.

Repeated prompt input need not mean repeated computation. Prefix caching enables the inference engine to retain and reuse previously computed Key-Value (KV) activations for prompt prefixes that remain identical across consecutive requests (Zheng et al. 2024). Reuse holds only for an exact token prefix computed under the same model and serving configuration, so altering an early system instruction or a tool schema forces fresh prefill from that point onward. The full compatibility conditions are developed in Prefix compatibility.

To see how much work prefix reuse saves, suppose the inference service caches earlier prompt prefixes and runs fresh prefill only on the newly appended turn suffix (the model’s previous reply plus the new tool observation). Across all \(\TurnHorizon\) turns, the prompt tokens needing fresh prefill drop to \(\BasePromptLength + (\TurnHorizon-1)\,\TurnAppendLength\). That baseline conservatively assumes the server does not keep the KV state produced during decode; engines that retain decoded KV state as well avoid fresh prefill on the earlier model replies too.

Napkin Math 2.2: The price of an append-only trajectory
Problem: A coding agent runs 30 turns. Its first prompt has 8,000 tokens, each turn appends 1,500 tokens, and each call emits 200 output tokens. Assume that every prompt fits the service’s context limit and that compatible earlier prompt state remains available for reuse. How much input does the trajectory supply, and how does prompt-prefix reuse change its estimated processing work?

Math: Applying the arithmetic series in equation 9 gives \(30\times 8,000+1,500\times\frac{30\times 29}{2}=892,500\) prompt tokens. Total output is \(30\times 200=6,000\) tokens. Without reuse, input therefore accounts for 99.3 percent of the combined input and output token count, although it does not account for that share of processing time.

With prompt-prefix reuse, fresh prefill covers \(8,000+29\times 1,500=51,500\) tokens. Dividing the original input count by this fresh-prefill count gives a 17.3× reduction. At the reference prefill floor, the prefill-work estimate falls from 63.68 s to 3.67 s. Decode work stays at 126.45 s; caching earlier input does not remove the task’s new responses.

Systems insight: Append-only context makes input work grow with the square of the horizon. Doubling the trajectory to 60 turns multiplies re-sent input by 3.5× but fresh prefill under prefix reuse by only 1.9×, because reuse converts quadratic growth into a linear trajectory. Prefix reuse therefore serves as the primary lever for minimizing the input component of \(\AttemptCost\) in the cost per accepted task (equation 1.2), with its value compounding as horizons lengthen. While caching preserves the full decision context without loss of information, cache eviction and cross-worker request routing can dilute these theoretical savings in production.

Even across the multi-turn trajectory above, decode time at the unbatched floor exceeds uncached prefill time, yet on a shared serving cluster uncached prefill can dominate the aggregate hardware work of that same trajectory. The two statements price different accounts. Wall-clock latency governs the responsiveness of a single trajectory. Because each turn depends on the outcome of prior tool executions, an agent cannot parallelize its own dependent turns, and every output token remains a sequential step on the critical path even when the server batches that step with other requests. Computational work, by contrast, sets cluster throughput, hardware provisioning, and aggregate operating cost. When a serving cluster batches dozens of concurrent streams, the weight reads at each decode step are amortized across all active requests in the batch. The amortized service time per decoded token then drops from the memory-bandwidth floor toward the compute-bound arithmetic limit \(2\Parameters/\PeakRate\) of equation 7, the same per-token efficiency as prompt prefill (Cost Asymmetry Between Input Prefill and Output Generation derives the batched-step bound). Once input and output tokens require comparable hardware work per token, total cluster work scales with aggregate token volume, which for an append-only agent is dominated by re-sent prompt history, provided the cluster sustains high batching and has memory for the attention state. Prefix reuse acts directly on that input component, while its effect on end-to-end latency depends on how much the input contributes to the call a consumer waits on.

Which term an engineer should attack therefore depends on who waits for the output, and the repair agent alone cannot show the contrast. Its consumer is a compilation and test pipeline that must wait for a syntactically complete response. The support agent instead streams output tokens to a person reading as they arrive. The consumer requirements determine which latency terms these two illustrated workloads prioritize.

Lighthouse 2.1: Call latency for two lighthouses
Support agent: A customer-operations turn submits an 8,192-token prompt (consisting of policy documentation, customer account history, and recent dialogue) and streams an 150-token response to a human user in a live chat interface. At the chapter’s reference hardware floors, prefill takes 0.58 s and generating the full reply requires 3.2 s. However, token streaming displays the initial words immediately following queueing and prefill; the remaining tokens arrive at approximately 47 tokens per second, faster than a person reads. Under these lengths and streaming rates, TTFT governs how soon the user can begin reading. When 6,144 prompt tokens represent a common prefix of system instructions, organizational policies, and tool definitions shared across all concurrent sessions, prefix caching leaves only 2,048 tokens of fresh prefill, which takes just 0.15 s. In a loaded cluster, admission queueing becomes the dominant contributor to first-token latency.

Repair agent: The initial repair turn commits 88.1 percent of its estimated hardware work to generating a 200-token response. The agent runtime can parse, validate, and execute the generated patch only after receiving the entire payload. Decode duration dominates this repair estimate, making the compactness of the requested code representation its primary optimization lever.

Systems insight: The illustrated support turn prioritizes TTFT because the user can read while the remaining tokens arrive, whereas output volume dominates the illustrated repair call, whose consumer waits for a complete patch. Shortening the support reply does not directly remove queueing or prefill from first-token latency. Caching the repair prompt reduces its input work and input cost, but that saving contributes little to this output-dominated estimate. Different prompt lengths, streaming rates, or serving load can change the binding term; measured phase durations and queueing determine which intervention improves the latency the consumer waits on.

Prefix reuse is not free. A cached prefix occupies accelerator memory that concurrent requests cannot use, and holding it through a long tool wait pins memory that no generation is using. Eviction discards only derived attention state, never the context itself, so the runtime can rebuild the prompt from its own logs at the cost of fresh prefill. Which evidence the model sees and whether its attention state is worth keeping are therefore separate decisions.

↪ The choice to keep, move, or drop attention state held through a tool wait is developed in Paused-State Policies.

Billed dollars form a third account, separate from latency and hardware work. Providers bill uncached input, cached input, and output tokens at different rates, and on-premise hardware amortizes fixed costs across utilization, so token-count ratios do not translate directly into dollars; Pricing prefix cache hits prices a whole trajectory under such tariffs. The per-call estimate is one building block of a task budget that also carries failed tool executions, retries, validation runs, and idle reservations during tool waits.

Checkpoint 2.3: Pricing calls and trajectories

A call’s token count and its elapsed execution time quantify distinct facets of the overall system budget.

Breaking down a call’s cost shows two main sources of waste: generating unchanged output tokens that the tool interface does not need, and recomputing prompt prefixes that the server could reuse. Cutting that waste helps only when the resulting output still solves the task, because a short diff that fails to apply or a cached prompt that produces a broken edit wastes the entire call. Choosing an output interface therefore requires comparing candidate accuracy, failure modes, and total resource spend under the same task budget.

2.9 Comparing Output Interfaces

When the repair agent proposes a fix to the connection-pool locking logic in src/pool.py, its output payload does not need to repeat the entire source file. The model could emit a fully rewritten file, or it could target a specific code block and emit only the replacement text. Emitting a localized edit consumes far fewer generation tokens by omitting unchanged code, but it creates an operational dependency: the execution runtime must locate the exact target coordinates in the target file. The interface therefore determines both the computational cost of generating a candidate and the operational preconditions required for the runtime to apply it.

High schema parse rates alone cannot validate an interface design. A whole-file replacement might parse into valid JSON yet arrive long after the service deadline because the model spent tens of seconds generating unchanged lines. Conversely, a compact diff might arrive in about 2.3 s yet fail to match its target anchors against current repository state. Neither outcome delivers an accepted repair. A rigorous empirical comparison must track the model call’s completion status, account for all compute resources consumed across retries, and validate the resulting artifact against uniform acceptance criteria.

2.9.1 Three choices in an output interface

When a patch interface fails in production by timing out on long files, missing its target lines, or breaking JSON syntax, teams often swap the entire prompt format at once and cannot tell whether edit granularity, wire framing, or decoding constraints caused the failure. Because those three choices are independent, diagnosing a failure or comparing designs requires varying one dimension at a time.

The first choice, edit granularity, sets how much text the model must emit to describe a change. At one end, a whole-file format returns the entire file, regenerating hundreds of untouched lines alongside the edit. At the other end, a localized edit supplies only a target locator and the replacement lines. An anchored search-and-replace block, for instance, gives an exact snippet of source text to find and the new text to substitute; if that anchor snippet is missing from the file or appears more than once, the runtime cannot apply the edit uniquely. Rewriting the whole file avoids anchor mismatches, but forces the runtime to check that the model did not accidentally delete unrelated functions or overwrite concurrent edits.

Regardless of which granularity the interface chooses, serialization framing remains an independent decision. Either edit size can be wrapped in JSON (one schema carrying a path and full file body, another carrying a path, search snippet, and replacement block) or framed with Markdown code fences or unified diff headers. Token overhead comes from the tokenizer’s vocabulary and the wrapper markers, not from whether the outer wrapper is JSON or diff syntax; emitting thousands of tokens for an unchanged file is the price of whole-file granularity, not of JSON.

How the runtime enforces that framing, the constraint mechanism, is independent of both granularity and wire syntax. An engineer can get structured outputs through fine-tuning, few-shot examples in the prompt, or grammar masking during decoding, and can combine them by running a fine-tuned model under a grammar mask. As section 2.7 showed, a grammar mask guarantees grammatical form, not valid targets or working code: it cannot ensure that an anchor snippet exists in the file or that the edit fixes the race condition. Changing serialization and decoding constraints at the same time tests a bundle of choices rather than isolating which change helped.

2.9.2 Matched-budget comparison

Giving a brittle diff format free retries whenever its output fails to parse, or dropping timed-out whole-file calls from the denominator, makes a weak interface look more accurate than it is. A fair comparison holds every condition fixed except the interface itself. In the connection-pool repair benchmark, every trial starts from the same repository snapshot and defect description, under the same model weights, sampling parameters, tools, acceptance tests, resource ceilings, and retry policy. Matched budgets do not require equal token consumption, because prompt overhead and response length are properties of the interface under test; they require identical operating limits and accounting rules.

↪ Matched-budget comparison of complete agent policies, charging every initiated trial to the cost per accepted task, is developed in Matched-budget comparison.

Two deadlines bound the comparison. The invocation deadline ends when the runtime receives and normalizes the model’s response. The task deadline also covers parsing, patching, compilation, and tests, and every retry, unparsable output, and failing run draws against it. A benchmark that grants free retries after malformed responses therefore subsidizes the brittle interface.

The harness also records each intermediate stage separately from final acceptance (table 5). Every initiated trial stays in the denominator of the top-level success rate, because discarding unparsable responses or timed-out calls inflates reported accuracy. Conditional rates, such as the share of applied edits that pass tests, remain useful diagnostics when they state their filter explicitly.

Latency measurements also need their serving conditions. Cache hits, batching contention, and queueing change observed wall-clock time at fixed prompt and completion lengths, so a few favorable measurements during quiet periods do not establish deadline reliability; tail latency decides deadline violations in shared clusters (Dean and Barroso 2013). The engineering question is whether a representation leaves enough headroom under the tail of the latency distribution to generate and validate a candidate fix.

Dean, Jeffrey, and Luiz André Barroso. 2013. “The Tail at Scale.” Communications of the ACM 56 (2): 74–80.

2.9.3 Choosing an edit format under a deadline

When a repair call must finish inside a fifteen-second deadline, asking the model to rewrite an entire source file spends almost the whole budget copying unchanged lines before a single test can run. The per-token floors of section 2.8.2 put a concrete price on that unchanged text, and that price falls on edit granularity rather than on the outer wrapper format.

Generating unchanged code, not reading the prompt, pushes the whole file past the deadline.

Napkin Math 2.3: A repair representation under an invocation deadline
Problem: A repair invocation begins with 8,000 baseline context tokens and operates under an invocation deadline of 15 s. Emitting a whole-file replacement requires 350 additional instruction tokens and 1,850 output tokens. Emitting a localized edit requires 120 additional instruction tokens and 80 output tokens. These token counts represent scenario parameters. Using the prefill and autoregressive decoding rates established earlier, which interface representation fits within the simplified service-work budget?

Math: The service-work model charges prompt prefill at 71.35 μs per token and autoregressive decoding at 21.075 ms per token. To isolate inference execution, this baseline calculation omits request serialization, network transit, server-side queueing, and subsequent validation runs. Multiplying each interface’s token profile by these unit costs yields:

\((8,000+350)\times 0.00007135\,\text{s}+1,850\times 0.021075\,\text{s}\approx 39.58\,\text{s}\)

\((8,000+120)\times 0.00007135\,\text{s}+80\times 0.021075\,\text{s}\approx 2.27\,\text{s}\)

The whole-file estimate exceeds the deadline by a wide margin, whereas the localized edit finishes well within the allocated budget. The localized representation provides a 17.5× reduction in estimated service work, with token decoding accounting for 98.5 percent of the whole-file latency. In production, normalized invocation outcomes and observed wall-clock times determine whether the candidate arrived before deadline expiration.

To evaluate downstream yield, consider a second hypothetical scenario where localized edits complete on time with valid syntax, but only 95 percent resolve to an applicable edit on the codebase. Among those applicable edits, assume 60 percent pass the required test suite. The fraction of all initiated trials that yield an accepted repair is:

\(0.95\times 0.60=0.57\)

This 57 percent yield is the product of the applicability rate and the conditional test-pass rate, requiring no independence assumption between stages. Invocations that miss deadlines or emit invalid syntax reduce this end-to-end yield even further.

Systems insight: When output generation threatens an invocation deadline, shrinking representation granularity is the primary engineering lever. Because autoregressive decoding is memory-bandwidth bound, each generated token costs roughly 295 prompt prefill tokens at hardware reference floors (principle 2). Whole-file generation squanders almost all of its 39.6 s runtime emitting unchanged code, while the localized edit meets the deadline with a 6.6× safety margin. However, compact representations trade decoding latency for applicability risk: localized edits require exact anchor matching, which restricts the passing yield to 57 percent in this scenario. That hypothetical yield forms the acceptance probability \(\AcceptanceProbability\) that governs the amortized cost per accepted task (equation 1.2).

The call deadline acts as a hard filter: under these timing numbers, a whole-file response misses the cutoff before any test can run, so its code quality never comes into play. If the deadline is relaxed past the whole-file duration, the trade-off flips toward applicability and patch accuracy. With a generous time budget, a localized edit format that frequently misses stale anchors or ambiguous line ranges can lose to a whole-file format that applies cleanly every time.

Checkpoint 2.4: Reconsidering the interface when conditions change

The repair interface must be reassessed whenever the workspace state or resource budget shifts.

Interface benchmarking joins the call contract to the cost of the call. The request specifies the context and output allowances; the normalized response confirms whether the model delivered a completed payload before the deadline; and tiered evaluation metrics determine whether the proposed mutation reliably solves the task within resource constraints. Assuming that a valid schema implies an applicable patch, or that a fast diff implies defect-free code, collapses these stages into one and hides which of them failed.

2.10 Fallacies and Pitfalls

A code repair request can return promptly yet name the wrong file, or return the right patch long after its latency budget has expired. In autonomous execution loops, engineers frequently conflate distinct operational boundaries, confusing inference execution with total round-trip latency, syntactic validity with semantic correctness, and transport success with task completion. These failures expose several persistent mistaken assumptions about the model call.

Fallacy: One model response has a fixed cost.

Output length drives most of the execution time in the unbatched interface comparison. At its reference hardware floors, decoding a single output token takes far longer than reading a prompt token in parallel. Regenerating a whole file takes 39.6 s, whereas emitting a localized patch for the same bug takes 2.3 s, a 17.5× speedup that comes almost entirely from generating fewer tokens (section 2.8). Engineers must therefore budget input and output tokens separately rather than treating a model call as a fixed unit of work. Wall-clock budgets must also cover queueing and network transit; setting max_output_tokens caps how many tokens the model generates, not when the reply arrives.

Pitfall: Treating a completed invocation as a completed task.

A status of COMPLETED says only that the serving engine finished generating tokens within the call’s limits; it does not mean the output is right or even usable. In the interface comparison, every localized edit finished on time, yet only 57 percent of those trials produced patches that passed the repair checks. Conversely, a call cut off at the token ceiling can still leave a syntactically valid file path, which is why the runtime must check completion status before acting on the payload. Every call record needs two separate facts: whether a complete candidate arrived from the service, and whether independent checks confirm that it satisfies the task contract.

Fallacy: Valid JSON means a safe and correct tool call.

A JSON object passes schema validation whenever its fields match the declared types, whether the payload is safe, authorized, or sensible. A tool call with a string path passes schema checks whether that path names the intended file, a stale file, or a sensitive directory outside the workspace. As table 3 shows, schema validation checks structure, while permission gates and task checks govern safety and correctness. The same gap holds for behavioral checks: a test runner with a false-accept rate of 0.65 on defective patches still leaves 14.37 percent of its passing candidates defective under the assumed rates. Even under grammar-constrained decoding, a call cut off at the token limit remains an unfinished fragment even though every token emitted before truncation was legal.

Pitfall: Collapsing every unsuccessful outcome into the same retry.

Retrying a failed call with the exact same request repeats the cause of the failure when the fault is not transient. If a whole-file request misses a 15 s deadline, resending the identical prompt spends another 39.6 s of estimated work only to time out again, while the first call may still be running and billed on the server, since a local timeout leaves the remote outcome unknown. Likewise, a call that hits max_output_tokens will truncate at the same limit on retry, and a safety refusal will refuse again. Instead of treating every failure like a dropped packet, the runtime should route on the normalized status: bounded backoff for transient service faults, a larger output allowance or more compact output format for truncation, and a fresh file read when an edit anchor fails to match. Recovery actions and evidence develops which recovery action fits each failure class.

↪ The idempotency rules that decide when a timed-out call may be repeated are developed in Retrying Uncertain Actions.

Fallacy: Greedy decoding makes a served call reproducible.

Greedy selection fixes the token choice for a given logit vector, but the serving infrastructure computes that vector, and two distinct causes can change it. The first operates even on a healthy serving path, where batch-dependent floating-point reduction order shifts closely contested logits in their least significant bits, enough to flip an argmax and redirect every later token. The second is a change to the serving path itself. In war story 2.1, misrouting and a miscompiled top-\(\TopTokenSampleCount\) operation changed model outputs for identical requests, affecting 16 percent of one model’s requests at peak impact, so an identical prompt succeeded on one attempt and failed on the next. Pinning the temperature addresses neither cause. Systems engineers should record the model revision and serving configuration with every invocation, and replay recorded responses whenever the goal is to reproduce downstream runtime behavior deterministically.

Each of these traps blurs two separate runtime records: token usage measures spend, completion status tracks whether generation finished, schema validation checks form, and acceptance tests check the result. Keeping those records separate lets higher-level loops compose single model calls without mistaking a clean response envelope for a working repair.

2.11 Summary

A repair agent interacts with a foundation model through a bounded request-response contract. The request bundles messages, tool schemas, token and time limits, and sampling settings into a serialized payload without handing any execution authority to the model. On the way back, the normalized status envelope separates completed proposals from truncated, refused, or failed calls, leaving the runtime to authorize side effects and verify results against the task contract. That division of work is invariant closure (principle 1) applied to a single model call, the baseline that every later chapter builds on. Table 6 summarizes the quantitative relations governing model invocations, tracking admission constraints, generation dynamics, verification limits, and trajectory-level resource scaling.

Key Takeaways: A model call is a bounded proposal interface
  • Budget the representation the service receives: Count serialized requests using the target model’s tokenizer and reserve explicit headroom for generated tokens. Context window limits, provider message caps, and network timeouts restrict distinct dimensions of the invocation; raw character counts never reliably predict actual token consumption or admission success.
  • Completion admits a candidate, not an action: Inspect the response kind and normalized status before consuming the payload. While an incomplete generation provides useful diagnostic data for debugging, syntactic parseability alone never promotes a partial string into a valid candidate, nor does it grant permission to execute tool actions.
  • Structural guarantees have a stated scope: Grammar constraints enforce syntactic validity under their schema assumptions, whereas permission checks govern permissible side effects and verifiers supply empirical acceptance evidence. A passing test supports only the properties exercised; model self-review remains a candidate assessment rather than independent confirmation.
  • Output and repeated input incur different costs: One output token costs about 295 prompt tokens at the reference hardware floors. Generation length dominates the illustrated unbatched repair estimate; prompt length and queueing can bind under other conditions. An append-only conversational history causes re-sent input tokens to scale quadratically with the trajectory horizon. Compatible prefix reuse turns that quadratic growth into linear work, but cached key-value states must never serve as the authoritative record of task state.
  • Compare accepted results within the same budget: Full-file replacements and localized patch edits trade generation volume against patch applicability constraints. Evaluate these alternatives by measuring end-to-end task completion, total token expenditure, and identical acceptance checks, rather than treating serialization format or schema parse rates as an engineering verdict.

2.12 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The foundation model call joins two intellectual lineages that developed independently for decades. The first is statistical language modeling. Shannon (1948) framed natural language as a discrete stochastic process, treating English as a sequence of symbols drawn from conditional probability distributions and generating text from successively longer context windows. Bengio et al. (2003) replaced high-dimensional count tables with a continuous neural function over distributed word representations, establishing the autoregressive next-token factorization that modern architectures still use. The Transformer made that formulation parallelizable across token positions during training (Vaswani et al. 2017). In production serving, that same architectural choice creates an operational split: an initial prompt prefill phase often limited by arithmetic throughput, followed by a small-batch autoregressive generation loop often limited by memory bandwidth (Pope et al. 2023). Subword tokenization algorithms borrowed from data compression solved the out-of-vocabulary dilemma (Gage 1994; Sennrich et al. 2016), and scaling laws subsequently produced models capable of task execution directly from natural language prompts (Brown et al. 2020). Bommasani et al. (2021) formalized the concept of foundation models while warning of their central systems risk: when hundreds of applications build on a single upstream base model, any flaw, bias, or failure mode in that model propagates throughout the entire software stack.

Shannon, Claude E. 1948. “A Mathematical Theory of Communication.” Bell System Technical Journal 27 (3): 379–423. https://doi.org/10.1002/j.1538-7305.1948.tb01338.x.
Bengio, Yoshua, Réjean Ducharme, Pascal Vincent, and Christian Jauvin. 2003. “A Neural Probabilistic Language Model.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 3: 1137–55.
Vaswani, Ashish, Noam Shazeer, Niki Parmar, Jakob Uszkoreit, Llion Jones, Aidan N. Gomez, Lukasz Kaiser, and Illia Polosukhin. 2017. “Attention Is All You Need.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 30: 5998–6008.
Brown, Tom B., Benjamin Mann, Nick Ryder, Melanie Subbiah, Jared Kaplan, Prafulla Dhariwal, Arvind Neelakantan, et al. 2020. “Language Models Are Few-Shot Learners.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 33: 1877–901. https://doi.org/10.48550/arxiv.2005.14165.
Fan, Angela, Mike Lewis, and Yann Dauphin. 2018. “Hierarchical Neural Story Generation.” Proceedings of the 56th Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics (Volume 1: Long Papers), 889–98. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/P18-1082.
Yu, Gyeong-In, Joo Seong Jeong, Geon-Woo Kim, Soojeong Kim, and Byung-Gon Chun. 2022. “Orca: A Distributed Serving System for Transformer-Based Generative Models.” 16th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI 22), 521–38.
Zheng, Lianmin, Liangsheng Ying, Zhenghao Yin, Cody Hao Liu, Eric Yu, Prakhar Patel, Zhipeng Yang, Anthony K. Joseph, and Ion Stoica. 2024. “SGLang: Efficient Execution of Structured Language Model Programs.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2312.07104, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2312.07104.

Decoding strategies evolved alongside model scale to mitigate systematic failure modes in generation. Early inference relied on greedy search or beam search to maximize likelihood, but these objectives frequently trapped models in bland, repetitive loops. Truncated sampling addressed this degeneration by restricting candidates to the head of the output distribution, first through a fixed token count in top-\(\TopTokenSampleCount\) sampling (Fan et al. 2018) and subsequently through top-\(\NucleusProbabilityMass\) (nucleus) sampling, which adapts the truncation boundary dynamically to the distribution’s cumulative probability mass (Holtzman et al. 2020). Speculative decoding decoupled generation latency from model parameter scale by using a lightweight draft model to generate token candidates that the larger target model verifies in parallel (Leviathan et al. 2023; Chen et al. 2023). At the infrastructure level, serving engines moved beyond rigid static batching: Orca introduced iteration-level scheduling to interleave prefill and decode phases dynamically (Yu et al. 2022), while SGLang demonstrated how prefix caching across requests amortizes repetitive prompt computations (Zheng et al. 2024). Together, these runtime innovations transformed the per-token computational costs analyzed in this chapter into shared, schedulable execution pipelines.

The second lineage stems from distributed systems engineering and access mediation across untrusted boundaries. When Birrell and Nelson introduced remote procedure calls, their goal was to make remote execution feel like a local function call, yet they showed that network boundaries expose failure semantics that local calls never experience, most notably states in which the caller cannot determine whether an operation ran (Birrell and Nelson 1984). Decades of systems architecture reinforce that lower-level abstractions cannot reliably guarantee end-to-end semantics; as the end-to-end argument demonstrates, true correctness must ultimately be validated by the participating endpoints (Saltzer et al. 1984). In an agent runtime, an external service returning a COMPLETED status serves as an execution hint rather than a definitive guarantee of success. Similarly, classical security engineering established the principle of complete mediation (Saltzer and Schroeder 1975), which requires validating every access request against current authority rather than trusting past authorization. An agent runtime must enforce the same discipline when dispatching model-proposed tool calls. Finally, parsing theory provides the formal prefix property (Knuth 1965) that underpins modern constrained decoding, ensuring token-by-token alignment with structured schema grammars (Scholak et al. 2021; Willard and Louf 2023). While sampling payloads dynamically across an open vocabulary is a modern capability, the defensive engineering required to govern those calls safely rests on these older systems foundations.

Scholak, Torsten, Nathan Schucher, and Dzmitry Bahdanau. 2021. “PICARD: Parsing Incrementally for Constrained Auto-Regressive Decoding from Language Models.” Proceedings of the 2021 Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing, 9895–901. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/2021.emnlp-main.779.

The following six papers provide essential reading on the conceptual and systems foundations of foundation model runtimes:

  • Birrell and Nelson (1984) introduces the mechanics of remote procedure calls and catalogs their failure semantics. It remains the classic reference for why invocation contracts across process boundaries must handle network partitions, cancellations, and undecidable execution states.
  • Saltzer et al. (1984) presents the end-to-end argument in system design, articulating why lower architectural layers cannot reliably guarantee higher-level application correctness. It underpins why an agent runtime cannot treat external service status codes (such as COMPLETED) as substitutes for independent outcome validation.
  • Holtzman et al. (2020) analyzes the degeneration of likelihood-maximizing decoding in open-ended text generation and introduces nucleus (top-\(\NucleusProbabilityMass\)) sampling. It demonstrates that decoding algorithms represent deliberate engineering trade-offs that govern output entropy, coherence, and candidate diversity.
  • Pope et al. (2023) develops an analytical roofline model of Transformer serving that separates compute-bound prompt prefill from memory-bandwidth-bound autoregressive decoding. It establishes the quantitative foundation for per-token latency floors, KV cache memory footprints, and batching trade-offs.
  • Willard and Louf (2023) introduces efficient constrained decoding by compiling regular expressions and context-free grammars into finite-state machines indexed over the model’s token vocabulary. It demonstrates how to enforce strict structural schemas at runtime with minimal per-token computational overhead.
  • Bommasani et al. (2021) surveys the technical capabilities and architectural risks of foundation models. Its analysis of model homogenization clarifies why centralized upstream dependencies require strict isolation boundaries, independent validation, and runtime guardrails across downstream applications.
Saltzer, Jerome H., David P. Reed, and David D. Clark. 1984. “End-to-End Arguments in System Design.” ACM Transactions on Computer Systems 2 (4): 277–88. https://doi.org/10.1145/357401.357402.
Holtzman, Ari, Jan Buys, Li Du, Maxwell Forbes, and Yejin Choi. 2020. “The Curious Case of Neural Text Degeneration.” Proceedings of the 8th International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR 2020).
Pope, Reiner, Sholto Douglas, Aakanksha Chowdhery, Jacob Devlin, James Bradbury, Jonathan Heek, Kefan Xiao, Shivani Agrawal, and Jeff Dean. 2023. “Efficiently Scaling Transformer Inference.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems (MLSys) 5: 606–24.
Willard, Brandon T., and Rémi Louf. 2023. “Efficient Guided Generation for Large Language Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2307.09702.

2.13 Exercises

All quantities below are hypothetical problem givens, chosen to be round, and describe no particular model, accelerator, or service. Where a problem uses the per-token work floors of equation 7, take them as floors in the chapter’s sense, which exclude queueing, transfer, attention-dependent work, and serving overhead.

1. Admitting a request with a long observation (Basic). A repair call runs against an interface with a combined context window \(\ContextWindow = 32{,}768\) tokens, and the runtime reserves \(\OutputLimit = 4{,}096\) tokens of output headroom. System instructions and tool definitions serialize to 3,200 tokens and the retained conversation to 18,000. The newest tool observation, a test log, was estimated at 9,500 tokens by dividing its 38,000 characters by four.

  1. Compute \(\SequenceLength\), the input ceiling, and whether the request is admitted. If not, find the largest observation that fits with the reservation unchanged.
  2. A teammate proposes halving \(\OutputLimit\) instead of shortening the log. Does the request then fit? Past proposals in this workload have run to 3,000 output tokens; state what the smaller reservation risks and which status the runtime would then record.
  3. The model’s tokenizer reports the log as 11,400 tokens, because indentation and escaped quotation marks split into many short tokens. Recompute both options, and state which measurement the runtime must take before any admission decision.

2. Where a call’s time goes (Basic). A dense model with \(\Parameters = 40\) billion parameters stores 8-bit weights (\(\ElementStorageSize = 1\) byte) on an accelerator with peak rate \(\PeakRate = 1{,}000\) TFLOP/s and memory bandwidth \(\Bandwidth = 4\) TB/s.

  1. Compute \(\PerTokenPrefillTime\), \(\PerTokenDecodeTime\), and their ratio.
  2. The first repair turn sends a 12,000-token prompt and generates a 300-token reply. Compute the prefill and decode times at these floors, the share of estimated work spent generating, and the share of tokens that are output.
  3. Two optimizations are proposed: trimming 3,000 tokens of retrieved documentation from the prompt, or a terser proposal format that halves the reply. Compute the work each saves and choose one. Then say how 4-bit weights (\(\ElementStorageSize = 0.5\) byte) would change the floor ratio and whether they change that choice.

3. How much diversity a setting buys (Basic). At one decision point a model’s logits over four candidate tokens are \(\LogitVector_t = [8, 7, 5, 2]\), and a later selection stage wants a first token that differs from the top-scoring one so that it has a genuine alternative to compare.

  1. At \(\SamplingTemperature = 1\), compute the four probabilities and the set retained by top-\(\NucleusProbabilityMass\) sampling with \(\NucleusProbabilityMass = 0.90\), with its renormalized probabilities.
  2. Compute the probability that one draw differs from the top token under (i) top-\(\NucleusProbabilityMass\) at unit temperature and (ii) untruncated sampling at \(\SamplingTemperature = 0.5\). How many independent draws does each setting need so that at least one differs from the top token with probability at least 0.9?
  3. An engineer concludes that the lower temperature is the “more accurate” setting because the top token receives more mass. Explain what the calculation does and does not show, and name what should choose the setting.

4. Paying for an append-only history (Intermediate). A repair trajectory runs \(\TurnHorizon = 40\) turns with \(\BasePromptLength = 10{,}000\) tokens and appends \(\TurnAppendLength = 2{,}000\) tokens per turn; each call generates 250 tokens. Hypothetical prices per million tokens are $3 for uncached input, $0.30 for cached input, and $15 for output.

  1. Compute the total prompt tokens supplied, the fresh-prefill tokens under prompt-prefix reuse (section 2.8), and the total output tokens. Repeat the first two for \(\TurnHorizon = 80\) and report each growth factor.
  2. Compute the trajectory’s dollar cost with no reuse and with every reused prefix billed as cached input. What share of the dollars is input in each case?
  3. A developer adds the current timestamp to the first line of the system prompt so the model knows the time. Compute the new cost, explain the mechanism, and redesign the request so the model still receives the time.

5. Ordering fallible checks (Intermediate). Suppose 25 percent of generated connection-pool patches violate the required concurrency property. Checker A (compile and unit tests) passes defective patches with \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate = 0.5\) and valid ones with probability 0.9, at $0.02 per run. Checker B (a concurrency stress test) has \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate = 0.2\) and passes valid patches with probability 0.8, at $0.30 per run. The task contract tolerates at most 5 percent defective patches among those accepted.

  1. Compute \(\Pr(\text{Defective} \mid \text{PASS})\) for A alone and for B alone. Does either meet the contract?
  2. Assume the two checkers’ verdicts are conditionally independent given a patch’s validity. Compute the residual for requiring both, the fraction of candidates accepted, and the expected checking cost per candidate when A runs first and B runs only on A’s passes, and when B runs first.
  3. Measurement shows that among defective patches that pass A, B passes 40 percent rather than 20 percent, because both miss races that the unit tests never schedule. Assume that the checkers’ verdicts remain conditionally independent for valid patches. Recompute the residual, state whether the pipeline still meets the contract, and propose one change to the checking design that attacks the correlation rather than adding another run of either checker.

6. Reject early, or never emit (Intermediate). A tool-calling service handles 10,000 proposals a day with prompts of 8,000 tokens and successful replies of 400 tokens, at \(\PerTokenPrefillTime = 80\) µs and \(\PerTokenDecodeTime = 10\) ms per token. Of these proposals, 6 percent violate the argument schema; the violation first appears at decode step 40 on average, and such a response otherwise runs 600 tokens before stopping. Every failed attempt is followed by one retry, which is assumed to succeed. Successful retries and completed grammar-constrained responses each contain 400 tokens. Measure added latency relative to one successful unmasked call; ignore cancellation signaling, validation, queueing, and transfer delays, and allow the retry to start immediately after local rejection. Three designs are proposed: validate after completion; validate the stream and cancel at the first illegal token (section 2.5); or grammar-constrained decoding, which removes schema violations but adds 0.3 ms of mask work to every generated token.

  1. For the two rejection-and-retry designs, compute the latency a failed first attempt adds before its retry can start and the expected added latency per proposal. For grammar-constrained decoding, compute the added latency from mask work instead.
  2. Compute the daily decode time the service spends on failed attempts under post-hoc validation and under streaming validation with an honored cancel. How does the second figure change if the service ignores cancellation, and which of the runtime’s guarantees survive?
  3. Find the per-token mask overhead at which grammar-constrained decoding ties streaming validation on expected latency. Then argue which design to ship, naming one property none of the three establishes.

7. The same method, a different binding term (Intermediate; second lighthouse). The research agent answers one question with 8 parallel workers. Each worker makes \(\TurnHorizon = 12\) calls, starts from \(\BasePromptLength = 6{,}000\) tokens, and on every turn appends a 5,000-token retrieved page plus its own 100-token output, so \(\TurnAppendLength = 5{,}100\). Use the floors and prices of problems 2 and 4, and ignore prefix reuse throughout.

  1. Compute the prompt tokens supplied per worker and across the question, the output tokens across the question, and input’s share of the uncached dollars.
  2. For a worker’s final call, compute the prefill and decode times at the floors. Which term of equation 6 binds, and how does that compare with the repair agent’s first turn in this chapter?
  3. One proposal copies the repair agent’s lever and halves every output to 50 tokens. Another keeps each retrieved page in full only for the call that reads it and replaces it afterward with a 400-token note. Compute the dollars each saves per question and choose one. What constraint of this lighthouse does neither calculation address?

8. Choosing a representation when the deadline moves (Advanced). A repair call starts from 9,000 context tokens, with the floors of problem 2 and the prices of problem 4. A whole-file response adds 300 instruction tokens and generates 1,200 output tokens; 98 percent apply to the expected version, and 60 percent of those pass the repair checks. A localized edit adds 100 instruction tokens and generates 120; 85 percent identify an applicable target, and 55 percent of those pass. Every attempt, whatever its outcome, also incurs a $0.05 check charge.

  1. Under an invocation deadline of 10 s, which representations can produce an eligible candidate?
  2. The deadline relaxes to 20 s. Compute \(\mathbb{E}[\AttemptCost]\), \(\AcceptanceProbability\), and \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) (equation 1.2) for each design, and the estimated model time per accepted repair. Which design wins on each measure?
  3. Find the applicability rate at which the localized edit ties the whole-file design on \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\). Then critique the benchmark: which of the two measures should decide for an agent whose trajectory has a wall-clock budget, what could raise the localized design’s applicability without lengthening its output, and what single measurement should be added before choosing?

9. A truncated command at the irreversible end (Advanced; second lighthouse). The remediation agent proposes 5,000 shell commands a month under an output allowance of 512 tokens, generated at \(\PerTokenDecodeTime = 10\) ms per token. Of these calls, 2 percent end TRUNCATED. Of the truncated fragments, 40 percent still parse as a complete command, as when --replicas=12 stops after --replicas=1. Executing a parseable fragment causes a wrong production change with probability 0.5, and each wrong change costs $20,000 to detect and reverse.

  1. Compute the expected wrong changes and loss per month for a runtime that dispatches anything that parses, and for one that applies the quarantining rule of section 2.5.
  2. For a truncated call, a blind retry at the same allowance truncates again with probability 0.9; a retry at a doubled allowance truncates with probability 0.1. Treating retries as independent and charging each attempt its full allowance as an upper bound, compute the expected attempts and decode time until a completed candidate under each policy.
  3. A colleague proposes a “repair parser” that closes truncated fragments so they can proceed without a retry. Critique the proposal using this lighthouse’s binding constraint, and redesign the runtime’s handling of TRUNCATED for this agent, stating what evidence a completed command still needs before it may execute.

What’s Next: From one candidate to deliberate computation
The contract ends at one prompt and one candidate, and a completed candidate can still rest on the wrong diagnosis. When the repair agent’s first patch extends a timeout that may not have caused the stall, the runtime can spend more before committing: a longer derivation, more sampled candidates, or a targeted test that observes the running pool. Each option buys a different kind of evidence, and only the test adds a fact the model did not already have. Test-Time Compute examines how to divide a limited budget among these options, how to select from a candidate pool without being fooled by a plausible failure, and when to stop.
